## Start here: high-priority items for review

Factual items confirmed by a second check and rated high: statements that could mislead a student's dissection or exam answer. Each shows the exact words from the notes. Please judge each one yourself: the AI can be wrong. Showing the 14 highest-confidence of 25; the rest are in section 1.

1. **Lecture 16 - Posterior Thigh and Popliteal Fossa** > Popliteal Fossa > Contents > Popliteal Artery
   - Notes say: "Continuation of femoral nerve past the adductor hiatus"
   - Why: The popliteal artery is the continuation of the femoral artery (an artery, not a nerve) as it passes through the adductor hiatus.
   - Basis: OpenStax §20.5 | confidence 0.96 | second check: confirmed

2. **Lecture 08 - Anterior and Medial Thigh** > Anterior and Medial Thigh > The Femoral Triangle > Femoral Artery
   - Notes say: "where it becomes the tibial artery"
   - Why: In the popliteal fossa the femoral artery becomes the popliteal artery, not a 'tibial artery'. The popliteal artery then divides into the anterior and posterior tibial arteries.
   - Basis: OpenStax §20.5 | confidence 0.95 | second check: confirmed

3. **Lecture 12 - Posterior Leg and Plantar Surface of Foot** > Popliteal Fossa > Contents > Popliteal Artery
   - Notes say: "Continuation of femoral nerve past the adductor hiatus"
   - Why: The popliteal artery is the continuation of the femoral artery (not a nerve) as it passes through the adductor hiatus. Calling it a nerve is a wrong structure type.
   - Basis: OpenStax §20.5 | confidence 0.95 | second check: confirmed

4. **Lecture 03 - Nerves Spinal Cord and Meninges** > Spinal Nerves
   - Notes say: "5 lumbar (C1 – C8)"
   - Why: The lumbar nerves are labelled L1–L5, not C1–C8. C1–C8 is the cervical designation, so this label is wrong and contradicts the 5 lumbar nerves stated in the same line.
   - Basis: OpenStax §13.4 | confidence 0.93 | second check: confirmed

5. **Lecture 08 - Anterior and Medial Thigh** > Anterior and Medial Thigh > Muscles of the Medial Compartment
   - Notes say: "All muscles innervated by obturator nerve"
   - Why: Not all medial compartment muscles are supplied solely by the obturator nerve. Pectineus is typically supplied by the femoral nerve (sometimes with an accessory obturator branch). The ischial (hamstring) part of adductor magnus is supplied by the tibial division of the sciatic nerve. 'All' is therefore misleading.
   - Basis: general anatomy knowledge | confidence 0.9 | second check: confirmed

6. **Lecture 08 - Anterior and Medial Thigh** > Anterior and Medial Thigh > The Femoral Triangle
   - Notes say: "Superior – inguinal canal"
   - Why: The superior border of the femoral triangle is the inguinal ligament, not the inguinal canal. The inguinal canal is a separate passage in the anterior abdominal wall above the ligament.
   - Basis: general anatomy knowledge | confidence 0.9 | second check: confirmed

7. **Lecture 12 - Posterior Leg and Plantar Surface of Foot** > The Foot > Muscles > 4th Plantar Layer > Plantar interossei
   - Notes say: "originating off the medial shaft of 3rd through 5th metacarpals"
   - Why: The plantar interossei are in the foot and arise from the metatarsals, not the metacarpals (hand bones). The 3rd to 5th metatarsals is correct.
   - Basis: general anatomy knowledge | confidence 0.9 | second check: confirmed

8. **Lecture 13 - The Hip and Knee Joints** > The Knee Joint > Joint Stability
   - Notes say: "Primarily due greater rolling of smaller medial femoral condyle"
   - Why: The medial femoral condyle is the larger one, and the lateral condyle is the smaller one. The lateral condyle finishes its rolling first, and the medial condyle continues to roll, which produces the rotation. The notes reverse which condyle is smaller.
   - Basis: OpenStax §9.6 | confidence 0.88 | second check: confirmed

9. **Lecture 16 - Anterolateral Thoracoabdominal Wall** > Slide 08
   - Notes say: "It’s nourished by the testicular artery, off the internal iliac, and has a rich sympathetic nerve supply"
   - Why: The ductus deferens is supplied by the artery to the ductus deferens, usually a branch of the inferior (or superior) vesical artery from the internal iliac. The testicular artery arises from the abdominal aorta, as the notes say earlier, so it cannot be 'off the internal iliac'. The statement is internally inconsistent and wrong.
   - Basis: general anatomy knowledge | confidence 0.88 | second check: confirmed

10. **ANA407 Class 28 - The Posterior Abdominal Wall** > Accessory Digestive Organs > Liver > part 2 of 2
   - Notes say: "augmenting hypoglycemia"
   - Why: In the fasted state, glucagon-driven release of stored glucose raises plasma glucose and counteracts or prevents hypoglycemia. 'Augmenting hypoglycemia' says the opposite and would mislead students.
   - Basis: general anatomy knowledge | confidence 0.85 | second check: confirmed

11. **Lecture 07 - The Antebrachium** > Bones of the Antebrachium > Ulna
   - Notes say: "Shaft terminates in ulnar head for articulation at the wrist joint"
   - Why: The ulnar head articulates with the ulnar notch of the radius (distal radioulnar joint). It does not articulate with the carpal bones, so it does not take part in the radiocarpal (wrist) joint. An articular disc separates it from the carpus.
   - Basis: OpenStax §8.2 | confidence 0.85 | second check: confirmed

12. **Lecture 07 - The Antebrachium** > Nerves of the Antebrachium > Radial Nerve > Deep Branch
   - Notes say: "Supplies branches to muscles of the posterior compartment of the arm"
   - Why: The deep branch of the radial nerve supplies muscles of the posterior compartment of the forearm (antebrachium), not the arm. Triceps (posterior arm) is supplied by radial nerve branches proximal to this point.
   - Basis: general anatomy knowledge | confidence 0.85 | second check: confirmed

13. **Lecture 08 - Anterior and Medial Thigh** > Anterior and Medial Thigh > Obturator Canal
   - Notes say: "Typically found at the medial margins of the membrane"
   - Why: The obturator canal lies at the superolateral margin of the obturator foramen, where the membrane is deficient beneath the obturator groove of the superior pubic ramus. It is not at the medial margin, so a student could misplace the canal.
   - Basis: general anatomy knowledge | confidence 0.85 | second check: confirmed

14. **Lecture 09 - The Hand** > Muscles of the Hand > Hypothenar Eminence > Opponens DigitiMinimi
   - Notes say: "Deep portion of the thenar eminence"
   - Why: Opponens digiti minimi is a hypothenar muscle (deep to the other hypothenar muscles), not part of the thenar eminence. The thenar eminence is the thumb's muscle group. The note sits under the Hypothenar Eminence heading, so 'thenar' appears to be an error.
   - Basis: OpenStax §11.5 | confidence 0.85 | second check: confirmed

## 1. Factual items for review

Factual items that an independent second check confirmed, most severe first.

**1. [HIGH · factual] Lecture 16 - Posterior Thigh and Popliteal Fossa**

- **Where:** Popliteal Fossa > Contents > Popliteal Artery
- **Notes say:** "Continuation of femoral nerve past the adductor hiatus"
- **Issue:** The popliteal artery is the continuation of the femoral artery (an artery, not a nerve) as it passes through the adductor hiatus.
- **References say:** The popliteal artery is described as the continuation of the femoral artery posterior to the knee.
- **OpenStax sentence:** "As the femoral artery passes posterior to the knee near the popliteal fossa, it is called the popliteal artery." (OpenStax A&P 2e §20.5)
- **Basis:** OpenStax §20.5 | **Confidence:** 0.96 | **Severity:** high
- **Second check:** confirmed: The popliteal artery is the continuation of the femoral artery through the adductor hiatus, so writing 'femoral nerve' is a factual error, not a matter of convention.

**2. [HIGH · factual] Lecture 08 - Anterior and Medial Thigh**

- **Where:** Anterior and Medial Thigh > The Femoral Triangle > Femoral Artery
- **Notes say:** "where it becomes the tibial artery"
- **Issue:** In the popliteal fossa the femoral artery becomes the popliteal artery, not a 'tibial artery'. The popliteal artery then divides into the anterior and posterior tibial arteries.
- **References say:** The femoral artery becomes the popliteal artery as it passes posterior to the knee. The popliteal artery then branches into the anterior and posterior tibial arteries.
- **OpenStax sentence:** "As the femoral artery passes posterior to the knee near the popliteal fossa, it is called the popliteal artery." (OpenStax A&P 2e §20.5)
- **Basis:** OpenStax §20.5 | **Confidence:** 0.95 | **Severity:** high
- **Second check:** confirmed: After passing through the adductor hiatus the femoral artery becomes the popliteal artery, which later divides into the anterior and posterior tibial arteries, so saying it becomes 'the tibial artery' is incorrect.

**3. [HIGH · factual] Lecture 12 - Posterior Leg and Plantar Surface of Foot**

- **Where:** Popliteal Fossa > Contents > Popliteal Artery
- **Notes say:** "Continuation of femoral nerve past the adductor hiatus"
- **Issue:** The popliteal artery is the continuation of the femoral artery (not a nerve) as it passes through the adductor hiatus. Calling it a nerve is a wrong structure type.
- **References say:** The femoral artery becomes the popliteal artery as it passes posterior to the knee.
- **OpenStax sentence:** "As the femoral artery passes posterior to the knee near the popliteal fossa, it is called the popliteal artery." (OpenStax A&P 2e §20.5)
- **Basis:** OpenStax §20.5 | **Confidence:** 0.95 | **Severity:** high
- **Second check:** confirmed: The popliteal artery is the continuation of the femoral artery as it passes through the adductor hiatus, so describing it as a continuation of the 'femoral nerve' is a factual error, as the flag says.

**4. [HIGH · factual] Lecture 03 - Nerves Spinal Cord and Meninges**

- **Where:** Spinal Nerves
- **Notes say:** "5 lumbar (C1 – C8)"
- **Issue:** The lumbar nerves are labelled L1–L5, not C1–C8. C1–C8 is the cervical designation, so this label is wrong and contradicts the 5 lumbar nerves stated in the same line.
- **References say:** The reference lists five pairs of lumbar nerves designated L1 to L5.
- **OpenStax sentence:** "five pairs of lumbar nerves designated L1 to L5" (OpenStax A&P 2e §13.4)
- **Basis:** OpenStax §13.4 | **Confidence:** 0.93 | **Severity:** high
- **Second check:** confirmed: The notes label the 5 lumbar nerves as 'C1 – C8', but lumbar spinal nerves are designated L1–L5 and C1–C8 is the cervical range, so the statement is an error and the flag is correct.

**5. [HIGH · factual] Lecture 08 - Anterior and Medial Thigh**

- **Where:** Anterior and Medial Thigh > Muscles of the Medial Compartment
- **Notes say:** "All muscles innervated by obturator nerve"
- **Issue:** Not all medial compartment muscles are supplied solely by the obturator nerve. Pectineus is typically supplied by the femoral nerve (sometimes with an accessory obturator branch). The ischial (hamstring) part of adductor magnus is supplied by the tibial division of the sciatic nerve. 'All' is therefore misleading.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.9 | **Severity:** high
- **Second check:** confirmed: The medial compartment is classically taught as obturator-innervated, but pectineus (femoral nerve) and the hamstring part of adductor magnus (tibial division of sciatic) are exceptions, so 'all' is an oversimplification that is clearly misleading, though pectineus is often placed in the anterior or medial compartment depending on the source.

**6. [HIGH · factual] Lecture 08 - Anterior and Medial Thigh**

- **Where:** Anterior and Medial Thigh > The Femoral Triangle
- **Notes say:** "Superior – inguinal canal"
- **Issue:** The superior border of the femoral triangle is the inguinal ligament, not the inguinal canal. The inguinal canal is a separate passage in the anterior abdominal wall above the ligament.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.9 | **Severity:** high
- **Second check:** confirmed: The superior (base) border of the femoral triangle is the inguinal ligament, and the inguinal canal is a separate passage in the abdominal wall, so the notes' statement 'Superior – inguinal canal' is an error even though the sub-bullet mentions the ligament.

**8. [HIGH · factual] Lecture 13 - The Hip and Knee Joints**

- **Where:** The Knee Joint > Joint Stability
- **Notes say:** "Primarily due greater rolling of smaller medial femoral condyle"
- **Issue:** The medial femoral condyle is the larger one, and the lateral condyle is the smaller one. The lateral condyle finishes its rolling first, and the medial condyle continues to roll, which produces the rotation. The notes reverse which condyle is smaller.
- **References say:** The lateral femoral condyle is slightly smaller than the medial condyle, so the lateral condyle finishes rolling first and the medial condyle follows.
- **OpenStax sentence:** "The rotation results because the lateral condyle of the femur is slightly smaller than the medial condyle." (OpenStax A&P 2e §9.6)
- **Basis:** OpenStax §9.6 | **Confidence:** 0.88 | **Severity:** high
- **Second check:** confirmed: The notes call the medial femoral condyle smaller, but standard anatomy has the medial condyle longer and larger in its anteroposterior arc, with the lateral condyle shorter; the screw-home rotation arises because the longer medial condyle keeps rolling after the lateral one stops, so the 'smaller medial condyle' wording is wrong.

- **Where:** Slide 08
- **Notes say:** "It’s nourished by the testicular artery, off the internal iliac, and has a rich sympathetic nerve supply"
- **Issue:** The ductus deferens is supplied by the artery to the ductus deferens, usually a branch of the inferior (or superior) vesical artery from the internal iliac. The testicular artery arises from the abdominal aorta, as the notes say earlier, so it cannot be 'off the internal iliac'. The statement is internally inconsistent and wrong.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.88 | **Severity:** high
- **Second check:** confirmed: The notes say the vas deferens is nourished by the testicular artery 'off the internal iliac', but the testicular artery arises from the abdominal aorta (as the notes themselves say earlier) and the vas is supplied by the artery to the ductus deferens, a branch of the inferior vesical artery from the internal iliac, so the statement is wrong and self-contradictory.

**10. [HIGH · factual] ANA407 Class 28 - The Posterior Abdominal Wall**

- **Where:** Accessory Digestive Organs > Liver > part 2 of 2
- **Notes say:** "augmenting hypoglycemia"
- **Issue:** In the fasted state, glucagon-driven release of stored glucose raises plasma glucose and counteracts or prevents hypoglycemia. 'Augmenting hypoglycemia' says the opposite and would mislead students.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.85 | **Severity:** high
- **Second check:** confirmed: Glucagon-stimulated hepatic glucose release raises blood glucose and counteracts hypoglycemia, so 'augmenting hypoglycemia' states the opposite of the physiology and is an error (likely a slip for 'preventing' or 'augmenting glycemia').

**11. [HIGH · factual] Lecture 07 - The Antebrachium**

- **Where:** Bones of the Antebrachium > Ulna
- **Notes say:** "Shaft terminates in ulnar head for articulation at the wrist joint"
- **Issue:** The ulnar head articulates with the ulnar notch of the radius (distal radioulnar joint). It does not articulate with the carpal bones, so it does not take part in the radiocarpal (wrist) joint. An articular disc separates it from the carpus.
- **References say:** The distal radioulnar joint lies between the head of the ulna and the ulnar notch of the radius. The distal radius, not the ulna, articulates with the proximal carpal bones.
- **OpenStax sentence:** "The distal end of the radius articulates with the proximal carpal bones, but the ulna does not." (OpenStax A&P 2e §8.2)
- **Basis:** OpenStax §8.2 | **Confidence:** 0.85 | **Severity:** high
- **Second check:** confirmed: The ulnar head articulates with the ulnar notch of the radius at the distal radioulnar joint and is separated from the carpus by the articular disc, so it does not take part in the radiocarpal (wrist) joint; the notes' statement is therefore misleading, though some texts loosely describe the ulnar head as being at the wrist.

**12. [HIGH · factual] Lecture 07 - The Antebrachium**

- **Where:** Nerves of the Antebrachium > Radial Nerve > Deep Branch
- **Notes say:** "Supplies branches to muscles of the posterior compartment of the arm"
- **Issue:** The deep branch of the radial nerve supplies muscles of the posterior compartment of the forearm (antebrachium), not the arm. Triceps (posterior arm) is supplied by radial nerve branches proximal to this point.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.85 | **Severity:** high
- **Second check:** confirmed: The deep branch of the radial nerve (continuing as the posterior interosseous nerve) supplies the extensor muscles of the posterior compartment of the forearm, while the posterior arm muscles (triceps) are supplied by radial nerve branches given off proximal to this point, so 'arm' is an error in a lecture on the antebrachium.

- **Where:** Anterior and Medial Thigh > Obturator Canal
- **Notes say:** "Typically found at the medial margins of the membrane"
- **Issue:** The obturator canal lies at the superolateral margin of the obturator foramen, where the membrane is deficient beneath the obturator groove of the superior pubic ramus. It is not at the medial margin, so a student could misplace the canal.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.85 | **Severity:** high
- **Second check:** confirmed: The obturator canal lies at the superolateral (superior) margin of the obturator foramen, beneath the obturator groove of the superior pubic ramus, so describing it as at the medial margin of the membrane is incorrect.

**14. [HIGH · factual] Lecture 09 - The Hand**

- **Where:** Muscles of the Hand > Hypothenar Eminence > Opponens DigitiMinimi
- **Notes say:** "Deep portion of the thenar eminence"
- **Issue:** Opponens digiti minimi is a hypothenar muscle (deep to the other hypothenar muscles), not part of the thenar eminence. The thenar eminence is the thumb's muscle group. The note sits under the Hypothenar Eminence heading, so 'thenar' appears to be an error.
- **References say:** The reference lists opponens digiti minimi among the hypothenar muscles, which form the hypothenar eminence at the little finger. The thenar muscles are the thumb muscles.

**15. [HIGH · factual] Lecture 09 - The Hand**

- **Where:** Long Tendons of the Hand > Flexor Digitorum Tendons
- **Notes say:** "Quervain tenovaginitis stenosans – fibrous thickening of sheaths and stenosis of osseofibrous tunnel due to excessive friction in tendons; may result in tendons getting “caught” in tunnel, producing painful snapping on flexion/extension (trigger finger)"
- **Issue:** De Quervain tenosynovitis involves the first dorsal extensor compartment (abductor pollicis longus and extensor pollicis brevis) at the wrist. It is not trigger finger. Trigger finger is stenosing tenosynovitis of the digital flexor sheath, typically at the A1 pulley. The notes conflate the two conditions.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.85 | **Severity:** high
- **Second check:** confirmed: The notes label trigger finger (stenosing flexor tenosynovitis at the A1 pulley) as 'Quervain tenovaginitis stenosans', but De Quervain tenosynovitis affects the first dorsal compartment (APL and EPB) at the wrist, so the flag is correct that the two conditions are conflated.

**16. [HIGH · factual] Lecture 14 - The Pelvic Cavity**

- **Where:** Neurovasculature of the Pelvic Cavity > Innervation
- **Notes say:** "Postganglonic cell bodies found within spinal cord segments S2-S4 (pelvic splanchnic division); travel to effector organs through ventral rami"
- **Issue:** Pelvic splanchnic nerves are preganglionic parasympathetic fibers. Their cell bodies are in the S2-S4 spinal cord segments, and they synapse in ganglia near or in the target organs. The postganglionic cell bodies are not in the spinal cord. The fibers also reach the organs via the inferior hypogastric plexus, not via ventral rami.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.85 | **Severity:** high
- **Second check:** confirmed: Pelvic splanchnic nerves carry preganglionic parasympathetic fibers whose cell bodies lie in the S2-S4 spinal cord gray matter, and postganglionic cell bodies sit in ganglia near or within the target organs, so calling them postganglionic cell bodies in the spinal cord is wrong.

**17. [HIGH · factual] Lecture 15 - The Perineum**

- **Where:** Neurovascular Supply to Perineum
- **Notes say:** "Physician palpates, targets the ischial tuberosity for injection site"
- **Issue:** The pudendal nerve block targets the ischial spine (transvaginal or transperineal approach), where the nerve passes around the sacrospinous ligament, not the ischial tuberosity.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.85 | **Severity:** high
- **Second check:** confirmed: The pudendal nerve block standardly uses the ischial spine as the palpated landmark, with the needle passing through or near the sacrospinous ligament, so naming the ischial tuberosity as the target is incorrect.

**18. [HIGH · factual] Lecture 03 - Nerves Spinal Cord and Meninges**

- **Where:** Vascularization of the Spinal Cord
- **Notes say:** "Cerebrospinal fluid from the subarachnoid space drains superiorly into the sulci of the brain"
- **Issue:** CSF is not drained into the sulci of the brain. It is reabsorbed into the venous blood of the dural venous sinuses (superior sagittal sinus) via arachnoid granulations/villi. The sulci are part of the subarachnoid space, not a drainage destination.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.8 | **Severity:** high
- **Second check:** confirmed: CSF in the spinal subarachnoid space does pass superiorly and into the cranial subarachnoid space (including the cerebral sulci), but its drainage out of the system is via arachnoid granulations into dural venous sinuses, so the notes' statement presents the sulci as the drainage endpoint, which is misleading.

**19. [HIGH · factual] Lecture 07 - The Antebrachium**

- **Where:** Posterior Compartment > Deep Layer > Extensor Indices
- **Notes say:** "Originates off the distal 2/3rds of the radius, interosseus membrane"
- **Issue:** Extensor indicis arises from the posterior surface of the distal ulna (distal third to two-thirds) and the interosseous membrane, not from the radius. The notes name the wrong bone of origin.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.8 | **Severity:** high
- **Second check:** confirmed: Extensor indicis arises from the posterior surface of the distal ulna (about the distal third to two-thirds) and the interosseous membrane, not the radius, so the notes name the wrong bone and the flag is correct.

**20. [HIGH · factual] Lecture 14 - Anterior Triangle of the Neck**

- **Where:** I. ANTERIOR TRIANGLE OF THE NECK > B. SUPRAHYOID REGION > Muscles
- **Notes say:** "the anterior belly is attached superiorly to the mental spine (or genial tubercle) of the mandible"
- **Issue:** The anterior belly of the digastric attaches to the digastric fossa of the mandible, near the midline on the inferior border. The mental spine (genial tubercle) is the attachment for the geniohyoid and genioglossus. The notes appear to have confused it with the geniohyoid attachment.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.

- **Second check:** confirmed: The anterior belly of the digastric attaches to the digastric fossa on the inner surface of the lower border of the mandible near the midline, not to the mental spine (genial tubercle), which is the attachment of geniohyoid and genioglossus, so the notes' statement is an error.

**21. [HIGH · factual] Lecture 16 - Anterolateral Thoracoabdominal Wall**

- **Where:** Slide 07
- **Notes say:** "The posterior wall of the inguinal canal is formed by the aponeuroses of the internal oblique and transversalis fascia."
- **Issue:** The posterior wall of the inguinal canal is formed by the transversalis fascia reinforced medially by the conjoint tendon. The conjoint tendon is formed by the internal oblique and transversus abdominis aponeuroses, not by the internal oblique and transversalis fascia. The notes name the wrong second structure (transversalis fascia instead of transversus abdominis).
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.

- **Second check:** confirmed: The posterior wall of the inguinal canal is formed by transversalis fascia, reinforced medially by the conjoint tendon, which is made of the internal oblique and transversus abdominis aponeuroses, so the notes' pairing of 'aponeuroses of the internal oblique and transversalis fascia' names the wrong second structure (transversalis fascia is a fascia, not a tendon or aponeurosis); the flag is correct.

**22. [HIGH · factual] Lecture 20 - Larynx**

- **Where:** VI. Muscles of the Larynx > C. Lateral Cricoarytenoid Muscles
- **Notes say:** "rotate anteriorly and laterally thereby approximating (ADDUCTING) and classing the glottis"
- **Issue:** The muscular process rotates anteriorly and MEDIALLY (not laterally) when the lateral cricoarytenoid contracts. This swings the vocal processes medially and adducts the vocal folds. Rotation laterally would abduct the glottis, as the posterior cricoarytenoid does, so the statement contradicts itself and the antagonism in item 4.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.8 | **Severity:** high
- **Second check:** confirmed: The lateral cricoarytenoid pulls the muscular process anteriorly (and slightly inferiorly), rotating the arytenoid so the vocal process swings medially; saying the muscular process moves 'laterally' is wrong and contradicts adduction, although 'classing' is just a typo for closing.

**23. [HIGH · factual] Lecture 08 - Anterior and Medial Thigh**

- **Where:** Anterior and Medial Thigh > The Femoral Triangle > Femoral Artery > Profunda Femoris Artery
- **Notes say:** "Transverse branch particularly important in supplying blood to the head of the femur"
- **Issue:** The femoral head and neck are supplied mainly by the medial femoral circumflex artery (via its retinacular/ascending branches), not the transverse branch of the lateral circumflex. Damage to the medial circumflex in femoral neck fractures is the classic cause of avascular necrosis. The transverse branch of the lateral circumflex is a minor contributor to the trochanteric anastomosis.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.7 | **Severity:** high
- **Second check:** confirmed: The statement is wrong as written: the medial femoral circumflex artery is the principal supplier of the femoral head and neck (via retinacular branches), while the lateral circumflex's transverse branch only contributes a minor share to the cruciate/trochanteric anastomosis.

**24. [HIGH · factual] Lecture 09 - The Hand**

- **Where:** Bones of the hand > Metacarpal Bones
- **Notes say:** "3rd metacarpal particularly vulnerable, due to its length"
- **Issue:** Boxer's fracture, the classic punching injury, involves the neck of the 5th (and 4th) metacarpal, not the 3rd. The 3rd metacarpal is firmly anchored and is not the typical site. The 3rd is the longest, but length is not why punching fractures occur. The 5th is more vulnerable because it is the most mobile and peripheral and has a thin neck.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.7 | **Severity:** high
- **Second check:** confirmed: The classic punching injury (boxer's fracture) involves the neck of the 5th (and sometimes 4th) metacarpal, because they are the most mobile and peripheral, while the 3rd is rigidly anchored at the carpus and is not the typical site, so the notes' claim is wrong.

**25. [HIGH · factual] Lecture 09 - The Hand**

- **Where:** Nerves of the Hand > Radial Nerve
- **Notes say:** "Superficial branch enters the hand superficial to the palmar fascia"
- **Issue:** The superficial branch of the radial nerve is a dorsal/posterior nerve. It passes from the forearm to the dorsum of the hand, running superficial to the extensor retinaculum or its border, and is not found on the palmar side. It does not enter the hand superficial to the palmar fascia. The notes appear to have confused palmar and dorsal, or confused the nerve with a palmar cutaneous branch (e.g. of the ulnar or median nerve).
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.7 | **Severity:** high
- **Second check:** confirmed: The superficial branch of the radial nerve reaches the dorsum of the hand by passing from beneath brachioradialis, piercing the deep fascia and crossing the anatomical snuffbox superficial to the extensor retinaculum, so it does not lie superficial to the palmar fascia; the notes' statement is wrong, though the flag's mention of the retinaculum's border is slightly loose.

**26. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 08 - Anterior and Medial Thigh**

- **Where:** Anterior and Medial Thigh > Femur: the Bone of the Thigh
- **Notes say:** "Small indentation in head called fovea centralis"
- **Issue:** The indentation on the femoral head is the fovea capitis. The fovea centralis is a structure of the retina.
- **References say:** The reference calls the indentation on the femoral head the fovea capitis.
- **OpenStax sentence:** "The fovea capitis is a minor indentation on the medial side of the femoral head" (OpenStax A&P 2e §8.4)

- **Second check:** confirmed: The pit on the femoral head for the ligament of the head of the femur is the fovea capitis femoris, while fovea centralis is the retinal region of sharpest vision, so the notes use the wrong term.

**28. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 01 - The Vertebral Column**

- **Issue:** Ankylosing spondylitis is a seronegative spondyloarthropathy, not a form of rheumatoid arthritis. They are distinct diseases. It mainly affects the sacroiliac joints, the enthesis sites and the spine, which can lead to fusion and a 'bamboo spine'. Calling it a form of rheumatoid arthritis could mislead students.

- **Second check:** confirmed: Ankylosing spondylitis is a seronegative spondyloarthropathy, distinct from rheumatoid arthritis (RF-negative, HLA-B27 associated, sacroiliac and enthesis involvement), so describing it as a form of rheumatoid arthritis is incorrect and misleading.

**29. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 07 - The Antebrachium**

- **Where:** Posterior Compartment > Outcropping Muscles > Extensor Pollicis Brevis
- **Notes say:** "Generates extension of wrist, MCP joint, interphalangeal joint"
- **Issue:** EPB extends the thumb's MCP joint (and abducts the thumb at the CMC joint, with a weak action at the wrist). It does not extend the interphalangeal joint; that is EPL's action, since EPB inserts on the proximal phalanx and so cannot cross the IP joint.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.8 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** confirmed: EPB inserts on the base of the proximal phalanx of the thumb, so it extends the MCP joint (and CMC) and cannot extend the IP joint, which is EPL's action; the notes' claim that it extends the interphalangeal joint is wrong.

**30. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 08 - Anterior and Medial Thigh**

- **Where:** Anterior and Medial Thigh > The Femoral Triangle
- **Notes say:** "permits communication between anterior compartment of leg and pelvis"
- **Issue:** The subinguinal space (retro-inguinal passage) connects the anterior compartment of the thigh, not the leg, with the abdomen/pelvis. The femoral vessels and nerve pass through it into the thigh.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.8 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** confirmed: The subinguinal space lies between the inguinal ligament and the pelvic bone and gives the femoral vessels and nerve passage between the pelvis/abdomen and the anterior compartment of the thigh, so writing 'leg' instead of 'thigh' is an error, especially in a lecture on the thigh.

**31. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 14 - Anterior Triangle of the Neck**

- **Where:** I. ANTERIOR TRIANGLE OF THE NECK > A. INFRAHYOID REGION > Muscular Triangle

- **Issue:** The thyrohyoid is not supplied by the ansa cervicalis. It is innervated by C1 fibres that travel with the hypoglossal nerve (nerve to thyrohyoid). The other three muscles (sternohyoid, sternothyroid, omohyoid) are supplied by the ansa cervicalis. Also, the ansa cervicalis is a nerve loop, not a single nerve.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.8 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** confirmed: The thyrohyoid is supplied by C1 fibres traveling with the hypoglossal nerve (nerve to thyrohyoid), not by the ansa cervicalis, so saying all the infrahyoid muscles are innervated by the ansa cervicalis is incorrect.

**32. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 15 - Face and Parotid Region**

- **Where:** I. Nerves to the Face > A. Trigeminal Nerve (Cranial Nerve V)
- **Notes say:** "Lacrimal Nerve – Supplies skin over forehead."
- **Issue:** The lacrimal nerve supplies the lacrimal gland, conjunctiva and the skin of the lateral upper eyelid, not the skin over the forehead. The forehead is supplied by the supraorbital and supratrochlear nerves.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.

- **Second check:** confirmed: The lacrimal nerve supplies the lacrimal gland, conjunctiva and skin of the lateral upper eyelid, not the forehead, so the notes' statement is incorrect as written.

**33. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 15 - Face and Parotid Region**

- **Where:** V. Facial Muscles > 4. Levator Labai Superioris
- **Notes say:** "Its action is to raise the angle of the mouth."
- **Issue:** Levator labii superioris elevates the upper lip, and it also helps to evert it and deepen the nasolabial furrow. Elevating the angle of the mouth is the action of levator anguli oris. A student could confuse the two muscles.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.8 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** confirmed: Levator labii superioris elevates the upper lip (and deepens the nasolabial furrow), while raising the angle of the mouth is the action of levator anguli oris, so the notes' statement attributes the wrong action to this muscle.

**34. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 17 - Cranial Fossa, Dura, Sinuses and Meninges**

- **Where:** I. Cranial Fossae
- **Notes say:** "Foramen lacerum - Internal carotid artery."
- **Issue:** In life the foramen lacerum is filled with cartilage and no major structure passes through it. The internal carotid artery passes through the carotid canal and only lies over the foramen lacerum. Listing the artery as a content of this foramen could mislead students.
- **References say:** The foramen lacerum is an artifact of the dry skull, filled with cartilage in life, and nothing passes through it.
- **OpenStax sentence:** "This opening is an artifact of the dry skull, because in life it is completely filled with cartilage." (OpenStax A&P 2e §7.2)
- **Basis:** OpenStax §7.2 | **Confidence:** 0.8 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** confirmed: In standard anatomy the internal carotid artery does not traverse the foramen lacerum, which is filled with cartilage in life and only has the artery lying on its upper surface (with minor structures like the nerve of the pterygoid canal passing through), so listing it as the foramen's content is misleading, though some textbooks loosely say it 'passes through' the upper part.

- **Issue:** Brachioradialis inserts on the lateral surface of the distal radius, just proximal to the styloid process, not on its anterior surface. It also passes over the anterior (flexor) side of the elbow, so describing the insertion as anterior is misleading.

**36. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 07 - The Antebrachium**

- **Issue:** Extensor digiti minimi inserts onto the extensor expansion (hood) of the 5th digit, on the dorsum of the proximal/middle phalanges, not the 5th metacarpal. The metacarpal is not its insertion site; the hood is over the finger (digit). Stating metacarpal is misleading and inconsistent with the next bullet about extending the 5th digit.

**37. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 08 - Anterior and Medial Thigh**

- **Where:** Anterior and Medial Thigh > The Femoral Triangle > Femora Canal
- **Notes say:** "Permits expansion of artery during systole"
- **Issue:** The femoral canal is the medial compartment of the femoral sheath, medial to the femoral vein. It contains lymphatics, fat and a deep inguinal lymph node (of Cloquet), and it does not hold the artery. Its standard function is to allow the femoral vein to expand, for example with increased venous return. The artery lies in the lateral compartment of the sheath, so the note ascribes the canal's function to the wrong vessel. A student could misread the canal as a space for the artery.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.72 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** confirmed: The femoral canal lies medial to the femoral vein and does not contain or neighbor the artery, so the standard teaching is that it permits the femoral vein to expand (venous return), not the artery during systole; the note assigns the function to the wrong vessel.

**38. [MEDIUM · factual] ANA407 Class 28 - The Posterior Abdominal Wall**

- **Where:** The Gastrointestinal Tract > General Histology > Muscularis
- **Notes say:** "Outer longitudinal layer – contract distal to ingested material to constrict GI tract proximally"
- **Issue:** The longitudinal layer contracts distal (ahead of) the bolus, shortening and widening the lumen there to receive the bolus. It does not constrict the tract; constriction is the role of the circular layer. 'Constrict GI tract proximally' is therefore the wrong function and misleading.
- **References say:** The reference says the longitudinal layer contracts to shorten the segment and push out its walls to receive the bolus, while the circular layer pinches the wall.
- **OpenStax sentence:** "At the same time, the longitudinal muscle layer of the muscularis also contracts, shortening this area and pushing out its walls to receive the bolus." (OpenStax A&P 2e §23.3)
- **Basis:** OpenStax §23.3 | **Confidence:** 0.7 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** confirmed: In peristalsis the longitudinal layer contracts distal to the bolus, shortening and widening the lumen to receive it, while the circular layer contracts proximal to the bolus to constrict and propel it, so the notes' claim that the longitudinal layer constricts the tract proximally is a wrong function.

**39. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 03 - Nerves Spinal Cord and Meninges**

- **Where:** Cells of the Nervous System
- **Notes say:** "Receptors found in effector organs, axon terminus in spinal cord"
- **Issue:** Sensory receptors are located in sensory organs or tissues (skin, muscle, viscera), not effector organs. Effectors are the targets of motor neurons (muscles, glands).
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.7 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** confirmed: The notes call the sites where sensory receptors lie 'effector organs', but effectors are the muscles and glands targeted by motor neurons; sensory receptors are in skin, muscle, viscera and other sensory structures, so the term is incorrect, although the notes may have meant 'peripheral organs' loosely.

**40. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 07 - The Antebrachium**

- **Where:** Bones of the Antebrachium > Ulna
- **Notes say:** "Styloid process increases concavity of wrist joint"
- **Issue:** The ulnar styloid process is a small posterior projection of the ulnar head. It anchors the connective tissue (triangular fibrocartilage complex) that unites the distal ulna and radius, and it does not contribute to the concavity of the wrist joint. The radial styloid process is the one that projects distally and limits lateral deviation at the wrist.
- **References say:** The ulnar styloid process is a short projection from the posterior side of the ulnar head. It serves as an attachment point for connective tissue that unites the distal ulna and radius.
- **OpenStax sentence:** "This serves as an attachment point for a connective tissue structure that unites the distal ends of the ulna and radius." (OpenStax A&P 2e §8.2)
- **Basis:** OpenStax §8.2 | **Confidence:** 0.7 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** confirmed: The ulnar styloid is a small posteromedial projection that anchors the triangular fibrocartilage complex and does not deepen the wrist's concave surface, which is formed by the radius and the articular disc; the notes also wrongly say the ulnar head articulates at the wrist joint, since the ulna is excluded from the radiocarpal joint by the disc.

**41. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 07 - The Antebrachium**

- **Where:** Nerves of the Antebrachium > Cutaneous Nerves
- **Notes say:** "Branches from the radial nerve in the forearm"
- **Issue:** The posterior antebrachial cutaneous nerve normally branches from the radial nerve in the arm (posterior compartment, in/near the radial groove), not in the forearm. It then descends to supply the posterior forearm skin. Saying it branches 'in the forearm' misplaces its origin.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.7 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** confirmed: The posterior antebrachial cutaneous nerve arises from the radial nerve in the arm, in or near the radial groove, and then descends to the posterior forearm, so the notes' 'in the forearm' misplaces its origin and the flag is correct.

**42. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 08 - Anterior and Medial Thigh**

- **Where:** Anterior and Medial Thigh > Femur: the Bone of the Thigh
- **Notes say:** "Partially bowed (concave medially) to absorb impact forces"
- **Issue:** The femoral shaft is bowed anteriorly, so it is convex anteriorly and concave posteriorly. 'Concave medially' misstates the direction of the bow.

- **OpenStax sentence:** "The elongated shaft of the femur has a slight anterior bowing or curvature." (OpenStax A&P 2e §8.4)

**43. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 09 - The Hand**

- **Where:** Bones of the hand > Carpal bones
- **Notes say:** "Articulate with radius, ulna through wrist joint proximally"
- **Issue:** The ulna does not directly articulate with the carpal bones. The wrist (radiocarpal) joint is formed by the distal radius and an articular disc (fibrocartilaginous pad), which separates the ulna from the carpals. Saying the carpals articulate with the ulna at the wrist joint could mislead students.
- **References say:** The distal end of the ulna does not directly articulate with any carpal bones. The radius articulates with the proximal carpals, and the triquetrum articulates with a fibrocartilaginous pad.
- **OpenStax sentence:** "The distal end of the ulna thus does not directly articulate with any of the carpal bones." (OpenStax A&P 2e §8.2)
- **Basis:** OpenStax §8.2 | **Confidence:** 0.7 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** confirmed: In standard anatomy the ulna is excluded from the radiocarpal joint by the articular disc of the triangular fibrocartilage complex, so the carpals articulate directly with the radius and the disc, not the ulna; the notes' statement that carpals articulate with the ulna through the wrist joint is a simplification that is technically inaccurate and misleading.

**47. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 12 - Posterior Leg and Plantar Surface of Foot**

- **Issue:** Popliteus unlocks the knee by laterally rotating the femur on the fixed tibia (open chain) or medially rotating the tibia on the femur (closed chain/non-weight-bearing). The notes say the tibia rotates on the fibula, but the rotation is relative to the femur; the tibia and fibula do not rotate on each other at the knee. The reference to the fibula is wrong.

- **Second check:** confirmed: The notes' wording 'medially rotating the tibia on the fibula' is wrong because the unlocking rotation of the popliteus occurs between the tibia and femur, not between the tibia and fibula, so the flag's criticism is valid, although the flag's own description of the open and closed chain actions is slightly muddled (it says the tibia medially rotates in the non-weight-bearing case, which is correct, and the femur laterally rotates in the weight-bearing case, which is also correct).

- **Notes say:** "Splits into 4 tendons that run obliquely to insert on long extensor tendons of digits 2 through 5"

Its tendons insert on the extensor digitorum longus tendons of digits 2-4, not digit 5. The notes give 4 tendons and digits 2-5, which is wrong; the fifth toe is served by the fibularis tertius/EDL.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.7 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** confirmed: In standard anatomy the extensor digitorum brevis (with the extensor hallucis brevis as its medial part) gives tendons to digits 1-4, with the hallux tendon inserting on the proximal phalanx and the other three joining the EDL tendons of digits 2-4, so the notes' 4 tendons to digits 2-5 is wrong, because digit 5 gets no EDB slip.

**50. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 12 - Posterior Leg and Plantar Surface of Foot**

- **Where:** The Foot > Muscles > Dorsal Compartment > Extensor Hallucis Brevis
- **Notes say:** "Extensor of 1st digit up to proximal IP joint"
- **Issue:** The great toe (1st digit) has only one interphalangeal joint, because it has just two phalanges (proximal and distal). Calling it the 'proximal IP joint' implies there is also a distal IP joint. Also, extensor hallucis brevis inserts on the proximal phalanx, so it extends the metatarsophalangeal joint, not the IP joint. It does not extend the IP joint, which is the action of extensor hallucis longus.
- **References say:** The reference states that the big toe has only proximal and distal phalanges, so it has a single interphalangeal joint.
- **OpenStax sentence:** "The big toe (toe number 1) has proximal and distal phalanx bones." (OpenStax A&P 2e §8.4)
- **Basis:** OpenStax §8.4 | **Confidence:** 0.7 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** confirmed: The notes' statement is wrong: the great toe has only one IP joint (two phalanges), and EHB inserts on the proximal phalanx so it extends the MTP joint, not the IP joint, which EHL extends; 'proximal IP joint' is therefore misleading.

**51. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 13 - The Hip and Knee Joints**

- **Where:** The Hip Joint > Femur: the Bone of the Thigh
- **Notes say:** "Partially bowed (concave medially) to absorb impact forces"
- **Issue:** The femoral shaft bows anteriorly, so it is convex anteriorly and concave posteriorly. 'Concave medially' misdescribes the direction of bowing.
- **References say:** The reference describes the femoral shaft as having a slight anterior bowing or curvature.
- **OpenStax sentence:** "The elongated shaft of the femur has a slight anterior bowing or curvature." (OpenStax A&P 2e §8.4)
- **Basis:** OpenStax §8.4 | **Confidence:** 0.7 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** confirmed: The femoral shaft is bowed anteriorly (convex anteriorly, concave posteriorly), so describing it as 'concave medially' misdescribes the direction of the bow, and the flag is correct.

**52. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 13 - The Hip and Knee Joints**

- **Where:** The Knee Joint > Intrinsic Ligaments
- **Notes say:** "Forms arch over popliteal ligament"
- **Issue:** The arcuate popliteal ligament arches over the popliteus tendon (the popliteus muscle), not a 'popliteal ligament'. This could mislead students about the structure.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.

- **Second check:** confirmed: The arcuate popliteal ligament arches over the popliteus tendon as it emerges from the joint capsule, so 'popliteal ligament' is a wrong or at best imprecise name for the structure it crosses, which could mislead students, though the notes' wording may be a loose shorthand.

**53. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 14 - The Pelvic Cavity**

- **Where:** Visceral Components of the Pelvic Cavity > Female Reproductive Tract > Uterus
- **Notes say:** "Provide support to the uterine artery"
- **Issue:** The transverse cervical (cardinal) ligaments support the cervix and upper vagina, and the uterine artery runs within them. They do not exist to support the artery. The stated function is wrong and could mislead students about what the ligament holds up.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.

- **Second check:** confirmed: The cardinal ligaments mainly support the cervix and upper vagina (and thereby the uterus), and the uterine artery merely runs within their upper part, so saying they 'provide support to the uterine artery' misstates their function.

**54. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 14 - The Pelvic Cavity**

- **Where:** Visceral Components of the Pelvic Cavity > Male Reproductive Organs > Testicles and Ductile Network
- **Notes say:** "segments of tunica albuginea invaginates, forming septa which divide testicle into 13-15 segments, each containing a seminiferous tubule"
- **Issue:** Septa divide the testis into roughly 250-400 lobules, not 13-15. Each lobule contains one to several coiled seminiferous tubules, not a single one.
- **References say:** OpenStax says the septa divide the testis into 300 to 400 lobules, within which sperm develop in seminiferous tubules.

- **Second check:** confirmed: Standard anatomy texts state that the septa divide the testis into about 250-400 lobules (Gray's/Moore cite roughly 250), each containing one to four coiled seminiferous tubules, so '13-15 segments, each containing a seminiferous tubule' is incorrect.

**55. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 14 - The Pelvic Cavity**

- **Where:** Neurovasculature of the Pelvic Cavity > Innervation
- **Notes say:** "Stimulate glandular secretions in both males and females during sexual arousal"
- **Issue:** Glandular secretions and vasodilation/erection during sexual arousal are parasympathetic (pelvic splanchnic) functions. Sympathetic fibers mediate emission and ejaculation. This item is listed under sympathetic functions.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.7 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** confirmed: Glandular secretions during sexual arousal (e.g., Bartholin's gland lubrication, prostatic and bulbourethral secretions) are classically parasympathetic via pelvic splanchnic nerves, while sympathetic fibers mediate emission and ejaculation, so listing this item under sympathetic functions is an error, although the bullet's indentation is ambiguous and some sympathetic glandular contribution to seminal fluid emission exists.

**56. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 16 - Anterolateral Thoracoabdominal Wall**

- **Issue:** The external oblique becomes aponeurotic at about the midclavicular line (lateral border of rectus abdominis) anteriorly, not the mid-axillary line. The mid-axillary line is much too lateral. Muscle fibers extend well anterior to it.

**57. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 16 - Anterolateral Thoracoabdominal Wall**

- **Where:** Slide 11
- **Notes say:** "allowing fusion of the anterior and posterior rectus sheaths"
- **Issue:** The tendinous intersections are fibrous bands that cross the rectus abdominis and adhere to the anterior layer of the rectus sheath only. They do not fuse the anterior and posterior sheaths; the posterior sheath stays free of them. Saying both sheaths fuse is anatomically incorrect.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.7 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** confirmed: Standard anatomy teaches that tendinous intersections are firmly attached to the anterior rectus sheath but not the posterior sheath, so the notes' claim that they fuse the anterior and posterior sheaths is incorrect, and the flag is right.

**58. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 16 - Posterior Thigh and Popliteal Fossa**

- **Where:** Posterior Thigh > Muscles > Biceps Femoris > Long Head
- **Notes say:** "Most important extensor of hip"
- **Issue:** The long head of biceps femoris is a hip extensor, but it is not the most important one. Gluteus maximus is the main hip extensor, especially for powerful extension such as climbing stairs or rising from sitting. The hamstrings are accessory or secondary extensors, mainly active in extension from a flexed or neutral position during walking. The statement could mislead students, and it is also duplicated.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.7 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** confirmed: The notes claim the long head of biceps femoris is the most important hip extensor, but gluteus maximus is the principal hip extensor in standard anatomy, so the flag correctly identifies an error (though some texts call the hamstrings major extensors in neutral/walking positions, the 'most important' claim is misleading).

**59. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 16b - Middle Ear**

- **Where:** II. External Ear > 7. Tympanic Membrane (ear drum)
- **Notes say:** "Perforation of the tympanic membrane causes deafness in the affected ear"
- **Issue:** A perforated tympanic membrane typically causes partial conductive hearing loss, not complete deafness. Overstated clinical claim.

**60. [MEDIUM · factual] ANA407 Class 28 - The Posterior Abdominal Wall**

- **Where:** Accessory Digestive Organs > Pancreas
- **Notes say:** "which break down protein into individual amino acids"
- **Issue:** Trypsin and chymotrypsin are endopeptidases that cleave proteins into smaller peptides, not individual amino acids. Final breakdown to amino acids needs other enzymes, such as carboxypeptidase and brush-border peptidases.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.

- **Second check:** confirmed: Trypsin and chymotrypsin are endopeptidases that cleave proteins into oligopeptides, and release of individual amino acids requires carboxypeptidases and brush-border peptidases, so the notes' statement is an oversimplification that is factually incorrect as written.

**61. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 01 - The Vertebral Column**

- **Issue:** The tectorial membrane is the upward continuation of the posterior longitudinal ligament and runs from the body of the axis (C2) to the inner surface of the occipital bone (clivus, anterior foramen magnum). It does not attach to the atlas as its vertebral attachment. Saying it connects the atlas to the occiput misidentifies its lower attachment.

**62. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 09 - The Hand**

- **Where:** Fascia and Compartments > Central Compartment
- **Notes say:** "Assists with tendon gliding"
- **Issue:** The midpalmar space is a potential fascial space (relevant to deep palmar infections) and does not facilitate tendon gliding. Gliding is provided by the synovial sheaths and ulnar/radial bursae. Attributing this function to the midpalmar space is misleading.

**63. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 12 - Posterior Leg and Plantar Surface of Foot**

- **Where:** The Foot > Muscles > 2nd Plantar Layer > Quadratus Plantae
- **Notes say:** "Paralysis of quadratus plantae one of the causes of pigeon-toe gate"
- **Issue:** Paralysis of quadratus plantae is not a recognized cause of pigeon-toe (in-toeing) gait. Loss of the muscle would lead to a slightly oblique pull of FDL and weaker toe flexion, not in-toeing. Pigeon-toe is typically caused by femoral anteversion, internal tibial torsion or metatarsus adductus. Also "gate" is a misspelling of "gait".
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.65 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** confirmed: Quadratus plantae straightens the oblique pull of FDL so the toes flex straight back, and its paralysis is taught as causing a slightly oblique, medially deviated toe pull, but pigeon-toe (in-toeing) gait is not a recognized result of this and is really due to femoral anteversion, tibial torsion or metatarsus adductus, so the notes' claim is misleading.

**64. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 14 - The Pelvic Cavity**

- **Where:** Overview
- **Notes say:** "Separated from abdominal portion through peritoneal covering"
- **Issue:** The pelvic cavity is continuous with the abdominal cavity and is not separated from it by a peritoneal covering. The boundary is the pelvic inlet (pelvic brim), a bony plane, and the peritoneum is continuous across it. Presenting the peritoneum as a separating partition could mislead students.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.65 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** confirmed: The notes' statement that the pelvic cavity is separated from the abdominal portion by peritoneal covering is anatomically wrong, since the abdominal and pelvic cavities are continuous across the pelvic inlet (a bony/imaginary plane) and the peritoneum is continuous across it rather than forming a partition; the following claim that pelvic organs lie retroperitoneal is also inaccurate, as many are subperitoneal or partly covered, so the flag is correct.

**65. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 14 - The Pelvic Cavity**

- **Where:** Visceral Components of the Pelvic Cavity > Male Reproductive Organs > Prostate Gland
- **Notes say:** "Alkaline fluid containing sugars, proteolytic enzymes to liquify coagulated semen"
- **Issue:** Prostatic fluid is not the source of the sugars (fructose) in semen; that comes from the seminal vesicles. The prostate contributes proteolytic enzymes (PSA), citric acid, acid phosphatase and zinc. Listing sugars as a prostatic component is misleading.
- **References say:** R1 attributes the fructose to the seminal vesicle secretions, not the prostate.

- **Second check:** confirmed: Prostatic fluid is slightly alkaline and contains proteolytic enzymes (PSA) that liquefy coagulated semen, but the fructose in semen comes from the seminal vesicles, so attributing sugars to the prostate is misleading, although some textbooks loosely list a few sugars in prostatic secretion.

**66. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 16 - Anterolateral Thoracoabdominal Wall**

- **Where:** Slide 08
- **Notes say:** "Typical ribs are broad and flat, with the long axis more or less running in a superoinferior direction."
- **Issue:** The long axis of a typical rib runs obliquely, curving anteroinferiorly around the thoracic wall (roughly horizontal/oblique), not superoinferiorly. A student could picture ribs as vertical.

**70. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 01 - The Vertebral Column**

- **Where:** Vertebral Column > Vertebral Morphology > Cervical Vertebrae > Axis
- **Notes say:** "commonly associated with spinal cord injury"
- **Issue:** Spinal cord injury and neurological deficit are relatively uncommon in a hangman's fracture, because the fracture widens the vertebral canal at C2 and decompresses the cord. Classic judicial hangings usually caused death by the distraction mechanism, but in modern traumatic cases (e.g. MVAs) neurological injury is not common. Saying it is commonly associated is misleading.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** confirmed: Standard teaching is that neurological deficit is uncommon in traumatic spondylolisthesis of the axis (hangman's fracture) because the fracture decompresses the canal, so 'commonly associated with spinal cord injury' is misleading, although judicial hangings with distraction did cause fatal cord injury.

**71. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 01 - The Vertebral Column**

- **Where:** Vertebral Column > Vertebral Morphology > Thoracic Vertebrae
- **Notes say:** "Tip of spinous process for each thoracic vertebra in line with body, IV foramen of inferior vertebrae"
- **Issue:** The 'rule of threes' for thoracic spines is not uniform. T1, T2 and T10-T12 spinous process tips lie at the level of their own bodies, and only T4-T9 reach the level of the body of the vertebra below. 'For each thoracic vertebra' is too general. Also, the spinous tip relates to the inferior vertebral body or the IV disc/foramen only for the mid-thoracic vertebrae.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** confirmed: The notes' blanket claim that every thoracic spinous tip lies at the level of the inferior vertebra's body/IV foramen ignores the standard rule of threes, in which T1-T3 tips lie at their own body level, T4-T6 at the level of the body below, T7-T9 roughly two segments down, and T10-T12 return to their own level, so 'for each thoracic vertebra' is overgeneralized, though the flag's exact grouping (T4-T9 as a block) is slightly different from the usual rule.

**75. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 07 - The Antebrachium**

- **Issue:** Deep branch (posterior interosseous nerve) compression spares the extensor carpi radialis longus (supplied by the radial nerve proper before the split), so wrist extension is weakened but there is no true wrist drop; the wrist deviates radially on extension. Fingers drop (finger drop), not a classic wrist drop. 'Drop-wrist' is misleading.

**76. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 08 - Anterior and Medial Thigh**

- **Where:** The Lower Limb > Overview
- **Notes say:** "Weight is redistributed from lateral to medial at the neck of the femur"
- **Issue:** Body weight is transmitted from the hip bone through the femoral head, which is medial, and then down the neck and shaft, so it moves from medial to lateral at the neck, not lateral to medial. The direction is reversed, which could confuse students about the force vectors and the bending stress at the neck.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** confirmed: Body weight passes from the acetabulum into the femoral head, which is medial, then laterally and inferiorly along the neck to the shaft, which lies lateral to the line of weight transmission, so the notes' lateral-to-medial direction at the neck is reversed, though the surrounding text on weight redistribution is loosely worded.

**77. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 09 - The Hand**

- **Notes say:** "Injury to any part of the median nerve results in loss of function of thenar eminence, progressing to ape-hand deformity without recovery"
- **Issue:** Injury to 'any part' of the median nerve does not cause thenar loss. Only injury at or proximal to the recurrent branch (e.g., wrist/carpal tunnel or higher) does; a lesion distal to the recurrent branch spares the thenar muscles. 'Without recovery' is also an overstatement, as nerve lesions can recover depending on the severity.

- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** confirmed: The statement is overgeneralized: the recurrent (thenar) branch arises at the distal carpal tunnel, so a median nerve lesion distal to it (e.g., in the palm or digital nerves) spares the thenar muscles, and 'without recovery' is an overstatement since median nerve injuries can recover, so the flag is correct.

**78. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 09 - The Hand**
