- **Where:** Posterior Thigh > Muscles > Biceps Femoris > Long Head
- **Notes say:** "Most important extensor of hip"
- **Issue:** The hamstrings, including the long head of biceps femoris, are hip extensors, but the gluteus maximus is generally regarded as the most important or powerful hip extensor, especially for climbing stairs and rising from sitting. Calling the biceps femoris long head the 'most important' extensor is misleading.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** confirmed: The notes call the long head of biceps femoris the 'most important extensor of hip', but gluteus maximus is the principal hip extensor in standard teaching, so the statement is misleading, though hamstrings are the main extensors during walking on level ground and some texts say they are the most important in unresisted extension.

**80. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 12 - Posterior Leg and Plantar Surface of Foot**

- **Where:** The Foot > Muscles > 2nd Plantar Layer > Quadratus Plantae
- **Notes say:** "Realigns pull of FDL more posteriorly"
- **Issue:** Quadratus plantae attaches to the FDL tendon and straightens its oblique line of pull so that it pulls along the long axis of the foot (more anteriorly toward the toes). It does not pull the tendon posteriorly; the FDL tendon passes the medial ankle and pulls obliquely medially, and the quadratus plantae corrects this by redirecting the force forward.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** confirmed: Quadratus plantae redirects the obliquely medial pull of FDL so it acts along the long axis of the foot toward the toes, so the notes' wording 'more posteriorly' is misleading, though the flag's own 'more anteriorly' description is a looser way to say the same thing.

**81. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 12 - Posterior Leg and Plantar Surface of Foot**

- **Where:** The Foot > Muscles > Dorsal Compartment > Extensor Digitorum Brevis
- **Notes say:** "Extensor of distal IP joints of digits 2 through 5"
- **Issue:** Extensor digitorum brevis, via its insertion on the long extensor tendons (extensor expansions), extends the metatarsophalangeal joints of digits 2-4 (and the great toe via extensor hallucis brevis). It is not described as a distal IP joint extender, and it does not serve digit 5. The statement is misleading.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** confirmed: Extensor digitorum brevis has no tendon to digit 5 (it supplies digits 2-4, with the hallux part being extensor hallucis brevis) and mainly extends the MTP joints, so the notes' claim of distal IP extension for digits 2-5 is incorrect on both counts, even though the notes' 4-tendon description is a common variant of textbook counts.

**82. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 14 - Anterior Triangle of the Neck**

- **Where:** I. ANTERIOR TRIANGLE OF THE NECK > A. INFRAHYOID REGION > Carotid sheath encloses 3 structures
- **Notes say:** "(2) Internal carotid artery"
- **Issue:** The carotid sheath in the neck encloses the common carotid artery (and, higher up, the internal carotid), not just the internal carotid. In the infrahyoid region of the anterior triangle the artery within the sheath is the common carotid artery. Listing only the internal carotid artery is misleading for this region.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** confirmed: The notes place the carotid sheath in the infrahyoid region, where the artery inside it is the common carotid (the internal carotid only begins at the level of the thyroid cartilage and continues upward within the sheath), so listing only the internal carotid is incomplete and misleading, though some texts loosely say 'internal carotid' for the sheath's contents.

**83. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 14 - The Pelvic Cavity**

- **Where:** Osteology > Pelvic Fractures
- **Notes say:** "Stable fractures – single fractures typically involve sacrum, coccyx inferior to pelvic ring"
- **Issue:** Sacrum and coccyx are not 'inferior to' the pelvic ring. The sacrum is part of the posterior pelvic ring, and only the coccyx lies inferior to it. Stable pelvic fractures are typically isolated single breaks of the ring, such as a pubic ramus or iliac wing fracture, or avulsion fractures. Describing the sacrum as outside the ring could mislead students about ring integrity.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** confirmed: The notes' wording is garbled, but the flag is largely right: the sacrum forms the posterior pelvic ring (only the coccyx lies inferior to it), and stable fractures are typically isolated ring breaks such as a pubic ramus or iliac wing fracture, so describing the sacrum as outside the ring is misleading.

**84. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 15 - Face and Parotid Region**

- **Where:** V. Facial Muscles > 6. Buccinator
- **Notes say:** "The parotid duct passes laterally and eventually pierces the buccinator muscle to enter the mouth."
- **Issue:** The parotid duct runs anteriorly across the masseter, turns medially at its anterior border, and then pierces the buccinator to open opposite the upper second molar. 'Laterally' is the wrong direction for the duct's course.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** confirmed: The parotid duct leaves the gland and runs anteriorly across the masseter before turning medially to pierce the buccinator, so 'passes laterally' is an inaccurate description of its course (it should be anteriorly, then medially), although the piercing of buccinator is correct.

**85. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 15 - The Perineum**

- **Where:** Urogenital Triangle > Deep Perineal Pouch
- **Notes say:** "Compressor urethra project anteriorly to urethra from ischial tuberosities to compress urethra against vagina when contracted"
- **Issue:** The compressor urethrae is a strap-like muscle that arches over (anterior to) the urethra and arises from the ischiopubic rami, not the ischial tuberosities. Saying it originates from the ischial tuberosities misstates its attachment.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** confirmed: In standard references (Moore, Netter, Gray's) the compressor urethrae arises from the ischiopubic rami and arches over the urethra, so the notes' 'ischial tuberosities' misstates its origin, though some texts loosely describe the attachment near the ischial tuberosities.

**86. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 15 - The Perineum**

- **Where:** Anal Triangle
- **Notes say:** "Open inferiorly posterior to transverse perineal muscles"
- **Issue:** The anal triangle is not described as open inferiorly; it is closed inferiorly by skin. Its ischioanal fossae are open anteriorly, where they extend above the perineal membrane/transverse perineal muscles into the anterior recess. The description of the direction of openness is wrong.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** confirmed: The ischioanal fossae are closed inferiorly by skin and open anteriorly via the anterior recess above the perineal membrane, so the notes' 'open inferiorly' is wrong, though the flag overstates slightly in its wording.

**87. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 15 - The Perineum**

- **Where:** Anal Triangle > Pelvic Diaphram
- **Notes say:** "Arises from anterolateral aspect of sacrum and coccyx, just inferior to piriformis"
- **Issue:** Coccygeus arises from the ischial spine (and sacrospinous ligament) and inserts onto the lateral margins of the coccyx and inferior sacrum. The notes reverse origin and insertion, listing the sacrum/coccyx as the origin and the ischial spine as the insertion.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** confirmed: Standard references (Gray's, Moore) give the coccygeus origin as the ischial spine and sacrospinous ligament, with insertion on the lateral coccyx and inferior sacrum, so the notes' statement reverses origin and insertion, though some teaching texts describe the attachments loosely or the reverse way.

**88. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 16 - Anterolateral Thoracoabdominal Wall**

- **Where:** Slide 13
- **Notes say:** "which supplies blood to superolateral aspects of the wall, anastomosing with decending branches off the intercostal arteries"
- **Issue:** The deep circumflex iliac artery runs along the iliac crest and supplies the inferolateral (iliac region) abdominal wall, anastomosing with the iliolumbar and lower intercostal/subcostal/lumbar branches. It is not superolateral. Also, the intercostal branches descend to the wall from above, which is not the artery's own territory.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** confirmed: The deep circumflex iliac artery runs along the iliac crest, so its territory is the inferolateral wall (iliac region), and it anastomoses with the iliolumbar, lumbar and lower intercostal/subcostal branches; 'superolateral' is a mischaracterization, though it does reach up the lateral wall along the crest and anastomoses with descending intercostal branches, so the error is arguable but real.

**89. [MEDIUM · factual] Lecture 20 - Larynx**

- **Where:** VI. Muscles of the Larynx > A. Cricothyroid Muscles
- **Notes say:** "Draws the arch of the cricoid posteriorly and superiorly"
- **Issue:** The cricothyroid tilts the thyroid cartilage forward and downward relative to the cricoid, or equivalently raises the anterior arch of the cricoid toward the thyroid. The arch of the cricoid does not move posteriorly. Its anterior arch is drawn superiorly, and the thyroid moves anteriorly and inferiorly. The posterior direction conflicts with the usual description of the action, which lengthens and tenses the vocal folds by increasing the distance between the thyroid angle and the arytenoids.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** confirmed: Standard texts (e.g. Gray's, Moore) describe the cricothyroid as drawing the cricoid arch superiorly and the thyroid anteriorly, or tilting the thyroid forward; the cricoid arch is not drawn posteriorly, and the notes' 'posteriorly' contradicts this and wrongly implies the arch moves backward, though some lecture sources use this phrasing for the arch rotating relative to the thyroid.

**90. [LOW · factual] Lecture 14 - The Pelvic Cavity**

- **Where:** Musculature > Levator Ani Muscle
- **Notes say:** "Inserts on coccyx, perineal body"
- **Issue:** Iliococcygeus inserts onto the anococcygeal raphe/ligament and coccyx; the perineal body is typically associated with pubococcygeus/puboperineal fibers, not iliococcygeus. Also pubococcygeus listing 'sacrum' as insertion is not standard (inserts on coccyx and anococcygeal body).
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** low
- **Second check:** confirmed: Iliococcygeus is standardly described as inserting on the coccyx and the anococcygeal raphe/ligament, and the perineal body is attached to by pubic-origin fibers (puboperineal/pubococcygeus), so listing the perineal body for iliococcygeus is inaccurate, though some sources vary in detail.

## 2. Worth a second look

Wording that could mislead students, then factual items the second check called doubtful (low priority).

**1. [MEDIUM · wording] Lecture 02 - Muscles of the Back**

- **Where:** Intrinsic Back Muscles > Superficial Group
- **Notes say:** "Bilateral contraction – ipsilateral flexion, rotation of neck"
- **Issue:** The first action line describes a unilateral action (ipsilateral lateral flexion and rotation), but it is labelled 'Bilateral contraction'. Bilateral contraction cannot produce ipsilateral movement, and the next line is also labelled bilateral. The first line should presumably read 'Unilateral contraction'. A student could be misled about the actions.
- **References say:** The reference says that acting individually (unilaterally), splenius capitis laterally flexes and rotates the head to the same side, and acting bilaterally it extends.
- **OpenStax sentence:** "Individually: laterally flexes and rotates head to same side; bilaterally: extension" (OpenStax A&P 2e §11.3)
- **Basis:** OpenStax §11.3 | **Confidence:** 0.9 | **Severity:** medium

**2. [MEDIUM · wording] Lecture 21 - Nasal Cavity**

- **Where:** IV. Arteries of Nasal Cavity (vascular supply from external and internal carotid…
- **Notes say:** "The anterior ethmoidal artery continues anteriorly to innervate the skin at the tip of the nose."
- **Issue:** Arteries supply (vascularize) tissue; they do not innervate it. The anterior ethmoidal artery gives the external nasal branch that supplies skin of the nasal tip (the anterior ethmoidal nerve innervates it). 'Innervate' is wrong for an artery.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.8 | **Severity:** medium

**3. [MEDIUM · wording] Lecture 13 - The Hip and Knee Joints**

- **Where:** The Knee Joint > Intrinsic Ligaments
- **Notes say:** "Continuation of patellar tendon beyond patella"
- **Issue:** The patellar ligament is the continuation of the quadriceps femoris tendon distal to the patella. The patella is a sesamoid bone within the quadriceps tendon, so calling it a continuation of the 'patellar tendon' is circular and misleading.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.7 | **Severity:** medium

**4. [MEDIUM · wording] Lecture 14 - Anterior Triangle of the Neck**

- **Notes say:** "Innervates all 4 muscles of muscular triangle (sternohyoid, omohyoid, thyroid and sternothyroid)."
- **Issue:** "thyroid" should be "thyrohyoid". There is no thyroid muscle, so the list as written is misleading. Also, the thyrohyoid is innervated by C1 fibers traveling with the hypoglossal nerve, not by the ansa cervicalis proper.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.7 | **Severity:** medium

**5. [MEDIUM · wording] Lecture 07 - The Antebrachium**

- **Where:** Posterior Compartment > Outcropping Muscles > Extensor Pollicis Brevis
- **Notes say:** "landmark for palpation of radial artery as it passes into the hand"
- **Issue:** The radial artery does pass through the anatomical snuffbox, so this is acceptable. However, the snuffbox is bounded by the tendons of EPL medially, and EPB and APL laterally. Saying the region lies 'between tendons' of EPL and EPB leaves out APL, a boundary tendon, which could mislead students.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.

**6. [MEDIUM · wording] Lecture 07 - The Antebrachium**

- **Where:** Arteries of the Antebrachium > Radial Artery
- **Notes say:** "muscles along the lateral aspect of the arm"
- **Issue:** The radial artery is in the forearm (antebrachium), so its muscular branches supply forearm muscles, not the arm (brachium). 'Arm' is the wrong region.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.65 | **Severity:** medium

**7. [MEDIUM · wording] Lecture 07 - The Antebrachium**

- **Where:** Arteries of the Antebrachium > Ulnar Artery
- **Notes say:** "Supplies numerous muscular branches to muscles along the medial aspect of the arm"
- **Issue:** The ulnar artery lies in the forearm (antebrachium), and its muscular branches supply the medial forearm muscles. The brachial artery and its branches supply the arm (brachium), so "arm" is misleading in a lecture on the antebrachium.
- **References say:** The ulnar artery forms at the bifurcation of the brachial artery, runs alongside the ulna, and supplies the lower arm (forearm) and carpal region.
- **OpenStax sentence:** "Formed at the bifurcation of the brachial artery; parallels the ulna; gives off smaller branches until it reaches the carpal region" (OpenStax A&P 2e §20.5)

**8. [MEDIUM · wording] Lecture 07 - The Antebrachium**

- **Where:** Nerves of the Antebrachium > Median Nerve > Anterior Interosseus Nerve
- **Notes say:** "Supplies deep compartment of muscles"
- **Issue:** The anterior interosseous nerve supplies only some deep anterior forearm muscles: flexor pollicis longus, the lateral (radial) half of flexor digitorum profundus (index and middle digits), and pronator quadratus. It does not supply the whole deep compartment, and the ulnar half of FDP comes from the ulnar nerve. The phrase 'deep compartment of muscles' could mislead students into thinking it supplies all of them.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.65 | **Severity:** medium

- **Where:** Nerves of the Antebrachium > Ulnar Nerve
- **Notes say:** "Passes between 2 heads of flexor carpi ulnaris, supplying motor branches to both muscles as it does"
- **Issue:** The ulnar nerve passes between the two heads of flexor carpi ulnaris (humeral and ulnar heads, under the cubital tunnel retinaculum/arcuate ligament) and supplies FCU. It does not supply "both muscles" here, since only one muscle (FCU) is involved at this point. The phrase "both muscles" is confusing and likely wrong. It may be a leftover from an edit, or it may mean the FCU and the medial half of FDP, which are mentioned in the next bullet.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.

**10. [MEDIUM · wording] Lecture 13 - The Hip and Knee Joints**

- **Where:** The Hip Joint > Hip Joint
- **Notes say:** "3 thickenings form intercapsular ligaments"
- **Issue:** The iliofemoral, pubofemoral and ischiofemoral ligaments are thickenings of the capsule, so they are capsular (intrinsic or extracapsular) ligaments. 'Intercapsular' is not the correct term. The intracapsular ligament is the ligament of the head of the femur.
- **References say:** The reference describes thickened areas of the articular capsule forming intrinsic ligaments, and the ligament of the head of the femur as intracapsular.
- **OpenStax sentence:** "The surrounding articular capsule is strong, with several thickened areas forming intrinsic ligaments." (OpenStax A&P 2e §9.6)
- **Basis:** OpenStax §9.6 | **Confidence:** 0.65 | **Severity:** medium

**11. [MEDIUM · wording] Lecture 14 - The Pelvic Cavity**

- **Where:** Osteology > Coccyx
- **Notes say:** "Posteroinferior portion of the pelvic girdle"
- **Issue:** The coccyx is not part of the pelvic girdle. The pelvic girdle is formed by the two hip bones. The coccyx, together with the sacrum, belongs to the bony pelvis and the axial skeleton. Calling it part of the pelvic girdle could mislead students. It is better described as the inferior end of the vertebral column, or the posteroinferior part of the bony pelvis.
- **References say:** The pelvic girdle is formed by the hip bones alone. The coccyx is a component of the bony pelvis, attached inferiorly to the sacrum.
- **OpenStax sentence:** "The pelvic girdle (hip girdle) is formed by a single bone, the hip bone or coxal bone" (OpenStax A&P 2e §8.3)
- **Basis:** OpenStax §8.3 | **Confidence:** 0.65 | **Severity:** medium

**12. [MEDIUM · wording] Lecture 15 - Face and Parotid Region**

- **Where:** I. Nerves to the Face > B. The Facial Nerve (Cranial Nerve VII)
- **Notes say:** "A motor nerve to the muscles of facial expression."
- **Issue:** Calling the facial nerve 'a motor nerve' is misleading. CN VII is a mixed nerve: its nervus intermedius carries taste from the anterior two-thirds of the tongue, parasympathetic fibers to the lacrimal, submandibular and sublingual glands, and a small somatic sensory component to the external ear. The notes later state that the facial nerve is 'purely motor', which a student could take to describe the whole nerve. The statement is true only of the five extracranial terminal branches in the face.
- **References say:** The reference lists the facial nerve among the mixed nerves with both sensory and motor fibers, and says it conveys taste and parasympathetic innervation of salivary glands.
- **OpenStax sentence:** "The remainder of the nerves contain both sensory and motor fibers." (OpenStax A&P 2e §13.4)
- **Basis:** OpenStax §13.4 | **Confidence:** 0.65 | **Severity:** medium

**13. [MEDIUM · wording] Lecture 16b - Middle Ear**

- **Where:** II. External Ear > 7. Tympanic Membrane (ear drum)
- **Notes say:** "and then to the cochlear nerve in the cochlea of the inner ear)."
- **Issue:** Ossicle vibrations are transmitted via the stapes at the oval window to the fluid (perilymph) of the cochlea, where hair cells transduce them. They do not pass directly to the cochlear nerve; the nerve carries the signal afterwards. Misleading simplification.
- **References say:** The reference describes the stapes attached to the oval window, vibrations creating fluid waves in the scala vestibuli and scala tympani of the cochlea, with sensory neurons transducing the signal.
- **OpenStax sentence:** "As vibrations of the ossicles travel through the oval window, the fluid of the scala vestibuli and scala tympani moves in a wave-like motion." (OpenStax A&P 2e §14.1)
- **Basis:** OpenStax §14.1 | **Confidence:** 0.65 | **Severity:** medium

- **Where:** VI. Muscles of the Larynx
- **Notes say:** "All muscles are innervated by the Vagus (CN X)."
- **Issue:** Overgeneralized if 'all muscles' includes the extrinsic muscles listed in the same section. Suprahyoid and infrahyoid muscles are supplied by the trigeminal, facial, hypoglossal and ansa cervicalis (C1-C3) nerves, not the vagus. Only the intrinsic laryngeal muscles are vagal. The statement appears before the extrinsic/intrinsic division and could mislead.

- **Notes say:** "Generally follow same path, given same names as accompanying arteries"
- **Issue:** This is true only for the deep veins (venae comitantes). The superficial veins of the brachium (cephalic and basilic) have no arterial counterparts and are not named after arteries. Without the qualifier a student may assume all brachial veins accompany arteries.

**23. [MEDIUM · wording] Lecture 01 - The Vertebral Column**

- **Where:** Vertebral Column > Overview
- **Notes say:** "Also protection for vena cava, aorta, trachea, esophagus; structures that would “kink” if not for proximity to vertebral column"
- **Issue:** The vena cava, aorta, trachea and esophagus lie anterior to the column and are not enclosed or protected by it in the way the spinal cord is. The trachea is supported by its cartilage rings, not by the vertebral column. Stating that these structures would kink without the column is misleading.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** medium

**24. [MEDIUM · wording] Lecture 01 - The Vertebral Column**

- **Where:** Vertebral Column > General Characteristics of the Vertebral Column
- **Notes say:** "Forceful extension of the neck (e.g. helmet tackle) can result in compression fractures within posterior columns"
- **Issue:** Spondylolysis is a pars interarticularis defect or stress fracture, typically lumbar (L5 most often) and from repetitive hyperextension. Linking it to neck extension and a helmet tackle is misleading. The text also describes the lesion as a 'compression fracture' within the posterior columns, which is imprecise for a pars fracture.

**25. [MEDIUM · wording] Lecture 01 - The Vertebral Column**

- **Where:** Vertebral Column > Joints and Articulations > Joints of the Vertebral Bodies
- **Notes say:** "compressing spinal cord, nerves"
- **Issue:** Disc herniation typically occurs posterolaterally and most commonly compresses the exiting/traversing spinal nerve roots at the intervertebral foramen. The spinal cord ends at about L1-L2, so lumbar herniations (the most common) cannot compress the cord. Saying the nucleus leaks into the vertebral foramen and compresses the cord is misleading.
- **References say:** The reference describes posterior protrusion of the nucleus pulposus compressing a spinal nerve where it exits through the intervertebral foramen, with no mention of cord compression.
- **OpenStax sentence:** "The posterior bulging of the nucleus pulposus can cause compression of a spinal nerve at the point where it exits through the intervertebral foramen" (OpenStax A&P 2e §7.3)
- **Basis:** OpenStax §7.3 | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** medium

**26. [MEDIUM · wording] Lecture 03 - Nerves Spinal Cord and Meninges**

- **Where:** Cells of the Nervous System
- **Notes say:** "Signal transduction at axon terminus"
- **Issue:** The axon terminus is where the signal is transmitted across the synapse (neurotransmitter release), not transduced. Transduction usually refers to receptors converting a stimulus into an electrical signal, so the term is misleading.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.

- **Issue:** The epidural space is the space between the dural sac and the vertebral walls (periosteum/ligaments). It is not simply the 'region of vertebral column external to the spinal cord', which would also include the subarachnoid space and the meninges. The statement is imprecise and could mislead students.

The acromioclavicular joint links scapula to clavicle. As written, it implies the AC joint is the link to the axial skeleton, which omits the sternoclavicular joint and could mislead students.

**35. [MEDIUM · wording] Lecture 07 - The Antebrachium**

- **Issue:** The interosseous membrane is a taut, fibrous sheet that binds the radius and ulna and transmits forces between them. **61. [MEDIUM · wording] Lecture 09 - The Hand**

- **Where:** Vasculature of the Hand
- **Notes say:** "often the brachial artery is compressed to prevent excessive bleeding during surgical repair"
- **Issue:** Because the hand is supplied by extensive radial-ulnar anastomoses (palmar arches), compressing only the brachial artery is not adequate. The brachial artery divides into the radial and ulnar arteries, so compression proximal to that point does stop inflow. The usual clinical teaching is that both the radial and ulnar arteries must be compressed at the wrist, because the anastomoses (and collateral flow) keep a lacerated hand bleeding when only one is occluded. The statement is misleading as written, since it does not explain this and the brachial artery is not the standard compression site for hand lacerations.

- **Where:** Anterior Compartment of the Leg > Muscles > Fibularis Tertius Muscle

- **Issue:** Fibularis tertius arises from the anterior (anterior/medial) surface of the distal fibula, often described as the distal third of the anterior surface, and the notes' 'medial surface' is imprecise. More importantly, the notes' proximal-distal relation to EDL is fine, but 'medial surface' is not the standard description; the fibularis tertius arises from the anterior surface of the fibula.

**63. [MEDIUM · wording] Lecture 10 - Anterior and Lateral Leg**

- **Where:** Lateral Compartment of the Leg > Muscles > Fibularis Brevis
- **Notes say:** "Forceful plantar flexion can result in avulsion fractures"
- **Issue:** The classic avulsion fracture of the base of the 5th metatarsal (pseudo-Jones/dancer's fracture) results from forceful inversion of a plantar-flexed foot, which puts tension on the fibularis brevis tendon. Fibularis brevis everts, so plantar flexion alone is not the mechanism. The wording could mislead students about the causative movement.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.

- **Issue:** The gluteal region is described as a transitional region between trunk and lower limb, but it is conventionally considered part of the lower limb (the gluteal muscles are muscles of the lower limb). Saying it is anatomically part of the trunk is potentially misleading.

- **Issue:** The sacroiliac joint is a largely immobile joint whose ligaments and interlocking surfaces serve to stabilise the pelvis and transmit weight. The following bullets describe tight interlocking, limited movement and suspension of the sacrum. This heading says the opposite: it implies the joint decreases rigidity, which contradicts the content below it. Possibly intended as 'increase rigidity/stability while allowing slight shock absorption'.

- **Issue:** The gemelli originate from the ischial spine (superior gemellus) and ischial tuberosity (inferior gemellus). They do not insert 'on' the obturator internus muscle; they blend with its tendon and insert together with it onto the medial surface of the greater trochanter. The wording could mislead students into thinking the muscle body is the insertion site.

The flexor hallucis longus tendon runs medially and the flexor digitorum longus tendons are in the central compartment, but the layer as a whole crosses compartment boundaries. The FHL tendon is associated with the medial compartment as it passes toward the great toe, so "entirely" is misleading.

It does not act at the interphalangeal joints, and the toes have a proximal IP joint but no flexion from this muscle there. The phrase 'up to proximal IP joint' is misleading about the joint crossed.

- **Issue:** The parotid is supplied by branches of the external carotid artery that traverse it: the external carotid itself, the retromandibular-level maxillary artery, the superficial temporal artery, and the posterior auricular and transverse facial branches. Naming only the superficial temporal arteries (plural) is incomplete and misleading. The external carotid artery is the main vessel passing through the gland.

- **Issue:** The secretory (parasympathetic) fibers originate from CN IX via the otic ganglion and merely hitchhike on the auriculotemporal nerve. Saying they are "from" the auriculotemporal nerve conflicts with the next sentence and implies a trigeminal origin.

It attaches to the fascia lata just below the inguinal ligament, which is a fusion/attachment, not a continuation. Calling it a continuation of fascia lata is misleading, and it also affects the spread of urine extravasation.

The sacrum/coccyx lie above the pelvic diaphragm, so saying they form the posterior limit of the anal triangle/fossa is misleading. The anal triangle's posterior limit is the coccyx, but the fossa itself is not bounded by the sacrum.

**83. [MEDIUM · wording] Lecture 16 - Anterolateral Thoracoabdominal Wall**

- **Issue:** Only the costal cartilages of ribs 1-7 attach directly to the sternum. Ribs 8-10 attach indirectly to the cartilage above, and ribs 11-12 do not attach at all. The statement implies all ribs' cartilages reach the sternum. Also the 'fuse' wording is imprecise, since most costal cartilages articulate with the sternum at synovial joints (only the first is a synchondrosis).

- **Where:** Slide 07

- **Issue:** Ribs 8-10 do not fuse into a single cartilage. Each costal cartilage attaches to the cartilage of the rib above, forming the costal margin, and this gives an indirect connection to the sternum. 'Fuse together' is misleading, since the cartilages articulate or connect with one another rather than fusing.

**88. [MEDIUM · wording] Lecture 16 - Anterolateral Thoracoabdominal Wall**

- **Issue:** The round ligament of the uterus (from the gubernaculum) attaches to the uterus near the uterine tube junction, passes through the inguinal canal, and ends in the labia majora. More importantly, the ovary is also anchored by the gubernaculum, forming the ovarian ligament (proper ligament of the ovary) and round ligament; the notes omit the ovarian ligament and imply the whole gubernaculum becomes the round ligament.

- **Issue:** A direct inguinal hernia is a protrusion of abdominal contents through a weakness in the posterior wall (Hesselbach's triangle), medial to the inferior epigastric vessels. It is typically an acquired attenuation or weakness of the transversalis fascia, not a tear in the conjoint tendon. The description is misleading.

- **Issue:** Indirect hernias through a patent processus vaginalis are congenital. Attributing them to older obese individuals is misleading; acquired weakness in older or obese people is more typical of direct hernias.

- **Notes say:** "Intraperitoneal organs – adventitia covered in visceral peritoneum, forming a serosa"
- **Issue:** Wording may mislead students: the serosa is the visceral peritoneum together with its underlying loose connective tissue. Saying the adventitia is covered by peritoneum and thereby forms a serosa blurs the distinction between adventitia and serosa as two different outer layers. Standard usage is that intraperitoneal organs have a serosa instead of an adventitia, while retroperitoneal or extraperitoneal organs and the esophagus have an adventitia.
- **References say:** R2 says the serosa is a layer of visceral peritoneum overlying loose connective tissue, and that the esophagus has an adventitia instead of a serosa. R1 says the esophageal adventitia is not covered by visceral peritoneum.

- **Basis:** OpenStax §23.1 | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** low

**120. [LOW · wording] Lecture 02 - Muscles of the Back**

- **Where:** Overview
- **Notes say:** "Innervated by ventral rami of spinal nerves"
- **Issue:** The extrinsic (superficial) back muscles are not all innervated by ventral rami of spinal nerves in the usual sense. Trapezius is supplied by the spinal accessory nerve (CN XI), and the rhomboids by the dorsal scapular nerve. Levator scapulae gets C3-C4 and the dorsal scapular nerve. Latissimus dorsi is supplied by the thoracodorsal nerve, a brachial plexus branch. The serratus posterior muscles get intercostal nerves (ventral rami). 'Ventral rami' is a broadly acceptable summary for most, but CN XI for trapezius makes the blanket statement imprecise and potentially misleading.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** low

- **Notes say:** "Runs lateral and inferior to insert on the lateral surface of the radius"
- **Issue:** The supinator wraps around the proximal radius and inserts onto the lateral surface of the proximal third of the radius. The description is acceptable, but "lateral surface" without context is imprecise as the fibres wrap around the posterior, lateral and anterior surfaces. Also, the origin omits the lateral epicondyle of the humerus, the radial collateral and annular ligaments, and the supinator crest of the ulna, so "posterior surface of the ulna" is incomplete.

**191. [LOW · wording] Lecture 07 - The Antebrachium**

- **Where:** Nerves of the Antebrachium > Median Nerve > Muscular Branches
- **Notes say:** "supply superficial, intermediate muscles (except flexor carpi ulnaris)"
- **Issue:** The median nerve's muscular branches in the forearm supply most superficial flexors (pronator teres, FCR, palmaris longus, FDS) and the intermediate layer, but the exception list is incomplete. Flexor carpi ulnaris is supplied by the ulnar nerve, and the ulnar nerve also supplies the medial half of flexor digitorum profundus (deep layer). Median nerve's deep muscles (FPL, lateral half of FDP, pronator quadratus) come via the anterior interosseous nerve, not these direct branches. The phrase 'except flexor carpi ulnaris' is acceptable, but the claim omits that the 'superficial' group is only supplied via direct branches by some muscles, and 'intermediate' (FDS) is fine; so the statement is mostly correct but the wording implies the median nerve supplies all other superficial and intermediate muscles, which is imprecise (e.g., brachioradialis is radial nerve and is superficial).

**200. [LOW · wording] Lecture 08 - Anterior and Medial Thigh**

- **Where:** Anterior and Medial Thigh > Os Coxae: the Pelvic Bone
- **Notes say:** "Fusion begins in late adolescence; complete by 25 years of age"
- **Issue:** The three components of the hip bone (ilium, ischium, pubis) begin to fuse at the acetabulum around puberty (roughly 14-16 years), not late adolescence. Fusion is usually complete by about 20-25. Stating that it begins in late adolescence is misleading, since the process starts earlier and finishes by the early 20s.
- **References say:** The text says the cartilage joins the ilium, ischium, and pubis during childhood and adolescence, and that when growth stops it is replaced by bone to form the single adult hip bone. It does not give a specific age at which fusion begins.

- **Issue:** The ischial ramus fuses with the inferior pubic ramus to form the ischiopubic ramus. Marked low confidence because the wording is not clearly wrong.

- **Issue:** The thumb is rotated about 90° relative to the other digits (nail faces laterally, the thumb lies in a plane perpendicular to the palm), and this positioning enables opposition. As worded, the 90° is attributed to a 'rotation' of the 1st digit, without saying relative to the other digits. Opposition itself is an active movement at the first carpometacarpal joint produced by opponens pollicis, not a result of the rotated orientation alone. The wording could mislead students.

**220. [LOW · wording] Lecture 09 - The Hand**

- **Where:** Fascia and Compartments > Palmar Fascia
- **Notes say:** "Wrist flexors (flexor carpi) found superficial to transverse ligament"
- **Issue:** Flexor carpi ulnaris lies superficial to the flexor retinaculum (attaching to the pisiform), but flexor carpi radialis passes through its own tunnel in a split of the retinaculum, lateral and deep to it. Calling both wrist flexors simply superficial is imprecise and could mislead students.

As written, it is vague and could be read as sitting between the thenar and interosseous compartments. The standard description is that the adductor compartment is deep to the central compartment, and the thenar compartment is lateral to it, not interposed with the interossei. **222. [LOW · wording] Lecture 09 - The Hand**

- **Where:** Muscles of the Hand > Thenar Muscles
- **Notes say:** "antagonistic muscles pull thumb into extension (ape-hand deformity)"
- **Issue:** Ape-hand deformity results from median nerve injury (thenar atrophy). The thumb is pulled into adduction and extension, so it lies in the same plane as the fingers. This is largely due to unopposed adductor pollicis (ulnar nerve) and the long extensor. Saying only 'extension' is imprecise, since the notes omit the adduction component. Also, 'deinnervation' is not named as median nerve injury, which could mislead.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** low

**223. [LOW · wording] Lecture 09 - The Hand**

- **Where:** Muscles of the Hand > Thenar Muscles > Opponens Pollicis
- **Notes say:** "flexion, internal rotation of 1st carpometacarpal joint"
- **Issue:** Thumb opposition at the first CMC joint combines flexion, abduction and medial rotation (pronation). The notes omit abduction. The phrase 'internal rotation of 1st carpometacarpal joint' is also loosely worded, since the rotation is of the metacarpal at that joint. Omitting abduction could mislead students about the components of opposition.
- **References say:** The reference describes opposition at the first carpometacarpal joint as a combination of flexion and abduction of the thumb.
- **OpenStax sentence:** "Thumb opposition is produced by a combination of flexion and abduction of the thumb at this joint." (OpenStax A&P 2e §9.5)
- **Basis:** OpenStax §9.5 | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** low

- **Notes say:** "Common origin off flexor retinaculum and carpal bones (pisiform, hook of hamate)"
- **Issue:** Wording: 'common origin' implies all three hypothenar muscles share the same origin. Abductor digiti minimi arises from the pisiform (and the tendon of flexor carpi ulnaris), whereas flexor and opponens digiti minimi arise from the hook of hamate and flexor retinaculum. The pisiform is not an origin for all of them.

**255. [LOW · wording] Lecture 12 - Posterior Leg and Plantar Surface of Foot**

- **Where:** Popliteal Fossa > Overview
- **Notes say:** "Affords bony protection anterior, soft tissue protection posterior"
- **Issue:** The notes imply that the fossa's contents are protected anteriorly by bone. The floor of the popliteal fossa is formed by the posterior femur (popliteal surface), the posterior knee joint capsule/oblique popliteal ligament, and popliteus fascia. The anterior side is therefore partly bone but largely capsule and muscle. The roof is skin and fascia, which gives little protection. Also, the popliteal vessels and tibial nerve lie in a fat-filled space and are not described as bone-protected in standard texts. The statement is imprecise and could mislead students.

- **Notes say:** "Wind tight during medial rotation; limited to 10⁰ (compared to 60⁰ lateral rotation) in flexed knee position"
- **Issue:** The cruciates wind around each other during medial rotation of the tibia, which limits it. The statement does not specify whether the rotation is of tibia or femur, which reverses the meaning. Medial rotation of the femur on a fixed tibia unwinds them, so the statement is ambiguous.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.

**300. [LOW · wording] Lecture 13 - The Hip and Knee Joints**

- **Where:** The Knee Joint > Menisci
- **Notes say:** "Distensibility/elasticity serve as shock absorption during foot strike with walking/running"
- **Issue:** Menisci are fibrocartilage and absorb shock through their compressibility and load distribution, not distensibility (stretchability). The term could mislead students about how they function.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.

**301. [LOW · wording] Lecture 13 - The Hip and Knee Joints**

- **Where:** The Knee Joint > Knee Injuries
- **Notes say:** "Infrequently torn when falling on flexed knee (pushes tibia backward)"
- **Issue:** The PCL is the ligament injured when a flexed knee is driven backward (tibia pushed posteriorly), e.g. dashboard injury. Calling this mechanism 'infrequent' is misleading. The PCL is torn less often than the ACL overall, but this is the classic mechanism and the wording could mislead.
- **References say:** Injury to the PCL occurs when the knee is flexed and the tibia is driven posteriorly, such as falling onto the tibial tuberosity or dashboard injuries. The text does not describe the PCL as infrequently torn in this mechanism.
- **OpenStax sentence:** "Injury to the posterior cruciate ligament occurs when the knee is flexed and the tibia is driven posteriorly" (OpenStax A&P 2e §9.6)
- **Basis:** OpenStax §9.6 | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** low

- **Where:** The Knee Joint > Knee Injuries
- **Notes say:** "Most commonly torn from compression forces of femur during valgus stressor"
- **Issue:** The medial meniscus is classically injured in the unhappy triad because it is attached to the MCL. Saying that the lateral meniscus is torn most commonly in valgus injury is contradictory with the standard teaching and the reference, but the notes also cite studies, so this is a possible conflict.
- **References say:** The textbook describes the sequence of injury from a lateral blow as tearing of the tibial collateral ligament, damage to the medial meniscus, and ACL rupture.
- **OpenStax sentence:** "tearing of the tibial collateral ligament, damage to the medial meniscus, and rupture of the anterior cruciate ligament." (OpenStax A&P 2e §9.6)

- **Issue:** The standard inferior boundary (apex) of the anterior triangle of the neck is the jugular (suprasternal) notch, i.e. the manubrium at the sternal notch, so "Sternum" is imprecise. Also, the standard boundaries are the base (inferior border of the mandible), the anterior border of SCM, and the midline of the neck. The notes' "Superior: Mandible" and "Inferior: Sternum" reverse the usual base/apex description. Verify the wording.

- **Issue:** The inferior belly of the omohyoid attaches to the superior border of the scapula near the suprascapular notch, and also to the superior transverse scapular ligament. **312. [LOW · wording] Lecture 14 - The Pelvic Cavity**

- **Where:** Osteology > Os Coxae: the Pelvic Bone
- **Notes say:** "Fusion begins in late adolescence; complete by 25 years of age"
- **Issue:** The notes say fusion only begins in late adolescence. The hip bone's three components are joined by the triradiate (Y-shaped) cartilage during childhood, and fusion begins around puberty (about 14-16 years), not late adolescence. It is usually complete by about 20-25 years, so the end point is acceptable, but the start is stated too late.
- **References say:** OpenStax says the cartilage joins the three parts during childhood and adolescence, and that the three bones fuse during the late teenage years.
- **OpenStax sentence:** "Each adult hip bone is formed by three separate bones that fuse together during the late teenage years." (OpenStax A&P 2e §8.3)

- **Issue:** The inferior pubic ramus projects inferolaterally, not simply laterally. Only the superior ramus passes laterally. The inferior ramus runs downward and laterally to join the ischial ramus. Saying both rami project laterally is imprecise and could mislead students about their orientation.
- **References say:** The superior pubic ramus passes laterally to the ilium, while the inferior pubic ramus extends downward and laterally from the body.

**318. [LOW · wording] Lecture 14 - The Pelvic Cavity**

- **Where:** Visceral Components of the Pelvic Cavity > Female Reproductive Tract > Ovaries
- **Notes say:** "Contains oogonia, which develop into oocytes and ultimately ova (eggs)"
- **Issue:** Oogonia develop into primary oocytes during fetal life, and oogonia are essentially absent in the postnatal ovary. The ovary of an adult contains oocytes arrested in meiosis, and the ovum forms only after fertilization triggers completion of meiosis II. Saying that the ovary contains oogonia is misleading.

- **Issue:** The buccinator arises from the alveolar processes of the maxilla and mandible (opposite the molar teeth) and from the pterygomandibular raphe. **340. [LOW · wording] Lecture 15 - Face and Parotid Region**

- **Notes say:** "to hold the cheek against the teeth and to keep the cheeks close against the teeth when exhaling."
- **Issue:** The two functions are redundant, and the second is misleading. The buccinator compresses the cheek against the teeth, which keeps food between the teeth during chewing. It also helps expel air when blowing, as the cheeks are compressed and air is forced out. 'Keep close against teeth when exhaling' misstates this role.
- **References say:** Describes the buccinator as compressing the cheek, allowing whistling, blowing and sucking, and contributing to chewing.

- **Issue:** The pelvic diaphragm supports the pelvic viscera, and indirectly the abdominal viscera, and resists raised intra-abdominal pressure. The phrase 'particularly in seated position' is misleading, because the diaphragm acts in the upright and standing posture, and it is the bony pelvis (ischial tuberosities) that bears weight when sitting. - **Issue:** The sentence is imprecise about the sequence. Internal sphincter relaxation (rectoanal inhibitory reflex) is involuntary and happens when the rectum distends. The notes' phrasing is ambiguous but consistent only if measured from the umbilicus. Because the standard description measures from the ASIS, a student could misplace it. **407. [LOW · wording] Lecture 16b - Middle Ear**

- **Where:** II. External Ear > 1. Auricle
- **Notes say:** "attached to the skull by skin, ligaments, muscles (auricular muscles) and the external acoustic meatus"
- **Issue:** The external acoustic meatus is not an attachment of the auricle in the same sense as skin, ligaments and muscles. The auricular cartilage is continuous with the cartilaginous part of the external acoustic meatus, so it is a continuity of cartilage rather than a separate attachment to the skull. A student could read this as the meatus anchoring the auricle to the skull.

**408. [LOW · wording] Lecture 16b - Middle Ear**

- **Where:** II. External Ear > 2. Ear Lobe
- **Notes say:** "Mostly fibrous tissue and blood vessels."
- **Issue:** The ear lobe (lobule) lacks the elastic cartilage that supports the rest of the auricle. It is made of areolar and fatty tissue with a rich blood supply, plus skin. Calling it 'mostly fibrous tissue' omits the fat and is imprecise. Fat is the main bulk of the lobule, so this could mislead a student about its composition.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.

**409. [LOW · wording] Lecture 16b - Middle Ear**

- **Where:** II. External Ear > 7. Tympanic Membrane (ear drum)

- **Issue:** The umbo is the depressed central point of the tympanic membrane when viewed from the external ear, but it is the tip of the malleus handle pulling the membrane inward, producing a convexity on the medial (middle ear) side. Calling it a 'concavity' is acceptable externally, so this is only a wording concern. However, the notes' description is mostly fine; the main risk is imprecision.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.

- **Notes say:** "Internal carotid artery (continuous with middle cerebral artery)."

The middle cerebral artery is not part of the circle of Willis. Listing the ICA as 'continuous with' the MCA is misleading. The ICA is the component that links the anterior and posterior parts of the circle via the posterior communicating artery. A student could wrongly think the MCA is a component of the circle.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** low

**421. [LOW · wording] Lecture 20 - Larynx**

- **Where:** I. Functions of Larynx
- **Notes say:** "Swallowing- Protects upper airway."
- **Issue:** The note says the larynx protects the airway, but it names swallowing as the function without saying how. During swallowing the larynx elevates and the epiglottis and laryngeal inlet close, which protects the LOWER airway (trachea and lungs) from aspiration. 'Upper airway' is imprecise and could mislead students, because the larynx is itself part of the upper airway and guards the entrance to the lower airway.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.

- **Issue:** The vallecula is a space/depression between the base of the tongue and the anterior (lingual) surface of the epiglottis, bounded by the median and lateral glossoepiglottic folds. It is anterior to the epiglottis rather than simply superior, and the notes do not say what structure it lies between.

**423. [LOW · wording] Lecture 20 - Larynx**

- **Notes say:** "Synovial joints which permits rotation and gliding movement."
- **Issue:** The cricothyroid joint is a synovial joint where the inferior horn of the thyroid cartilage articulates with the cricoid. Its movement is rotation (tilting) of the thyroid cartilage about a transverse axis, with some slight gliding. The notes list this joint's movement the same way as the cricoarytenoid joint. The cricoarytenoid joint's rotation and gliding is a different movement, so the notes may blur the two joints' actions and their effect on vocal fold tension versus abduction/adduction. This is imprecise rather than wrong.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.

- **Issue:** In standard anatomy the free superior border of the quadrangular membrane forms the aryepiglottic ligament, which is covered by mucosa to make the aryepiglottic fold. Saying the border itself forms the fold is imprecise.

Stating only the inferior horn is incomplete and could mislead students, who may miss the lower border of the lamina as an insertion site.

**428. [LOW · wording] Lecture 20 - Larynx**

- **Issue:** Posterior cricoarytenoid contraction pulls the muscular process posteriorly and medially, and rotates the arytenoid so the vocal process swings laterally and upward. 'Down' is wrong: the muscular process moves medially and the vocal process moves up and laterally. The usual description is that the muscular process is pulled medially and posteriorly, rotating the arytenoid to abduct the vocal fold.

**429. [LOW · wording] Lecture 20 - Larynx**

- **Issue:** The aryepiglottic muscles (continuations of the oblique arytenoids) run in the aryepiglottic folds and act as a sphincter of the laryngeal inlet. By drawing the epiglottis toward the arytenoids they pull it down and back, covering the inlet. Saying only 'backward-posteriorly' omits the downward (inferior) movement and the sphincteric closure of the inlet. A student may picture a purely posterior movement and miss the inlet-closing function. Wording is imprecise rather than clearly wrong.

Saying only 'originates' with no insertion on the arytenoid is incomplete, and the insertion given is only the vocal ligament, which is partly correct since fibres attach to the vocal ligament and the vocal process of the arytenoid. This is probably acceptable, so this flag is low confidence.

- **Issue:** Laryngeal veins do not simply follow the thyroid arteries. The superior laryngeal vein drains to the superior thyroid vein, then the internal jugular or facial vein. The inferior laryngeal vein drains to the inferior thyroid vein, then the brachiocephalic vein. The statement is imprecise because it names arteries rather than the corresponding veins and leaves out the drainage destinations. A student could be misled about venous drainage.

**443. [MEDIUM · factual · doubtful, low priority] Lecture 07 - The Antebrachium**

- **Issue:** The supinator is the muscle that supinates; its fibres wrap around the proximal radius and unwind it into supination. Saying it corkscrews "during pronation" contradicts the next bullet, which says it supinates, and would mislead students about its action. The pronators are pronator teres and pronator quadratus.

- **Basis:** OpenStax §11.5 | **Confidence:** 0.8 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** doubtful: The supinator wraps around the proximal radius and its contraction unwinds it into supination, so saying it 'corkscrews during pronation' is arguably a misstatement of its action, though it could be read as the muscle being wound up (stretched) around the radius during pronation, which is not standard teaching and contradicts the next bullet.

**444. [MEDIUM · factual · doubtful, low priority] Lecture 16 - Anterolateral Thoracoabdominal Wall**

- **Where:** Slide 10
- **Notes say:** "ribs 1 through 10 communicate with the sternum through the costal cartilages"
- **Issue:** Ribs 8-10 do not reach the sternum; their cartilages join the cartilage of the rib above. Only ribs 1-7 attach directly to the sternum, so this statement is wrong or misleading.
- **References say:** Ribs 1-7 attach directly to the sternum via costal cartilage; ribs 8-10 attach to the cartilage of the next higher rib, not to the sternum.

- **Second check:** doubtful: Ribs 1-7 attach directly to the sternum via their own costal cartilages, while ribs 8-10 connect only indirectly through the cartilage of the rib above, so saying ribs 1-10 communicate with the sternum 'through the costal cartilages' is loosely acceptable if indirect, but the notes' later claim that fused cartilages of 7-10 'reach the sternum' is imprecise, making this statement arguably acceptable.

- **Issue:** The patella articulates with the patellar surface of the femur, which lies on the anterior distal femur. The intercondylar region (fossa) is on the posterior/inferior femur and does not articulate with the patella. 'Anterior intercondylar surface' is the wrong name for this articular area.

- **Second check:** doubtful: The patella articulates with the patellar surface of the femur, which lies anteriorly between the condyles, and some lecture notes loosely call it the anterior intercondylar surface, so the term is nonstandard but not clearly wrong, though 'intercondylar' risks confusion with the posterior intercondylar fossa.

**446. [MEDIUM · factual · doubtful, low priority] Lecture 03 - Nerves Spinal Cord and Meninges**

- **Where:** Spinal Cord > Spinal Cord in Cross Section

- **Issue:** Lateral horns (intermediolateral cell column) contain sympathetic preganglionic neurons and are present from T1 to L2/L3, not only in the lower thoracic region. Saying 'autonomic' also omits that the sacral cord (S2-S4) has parasympathetic preganglionic cell bodies in an equivalent position.

- **Basis:** OpenStax §13.2 | **Confidence:** 0.65 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** doubtful: The notes say lateral horns are present in 'lower thoracic, upper lumbar' regions, which is a simplification of the T1-L2/L3 sympathetic column, and 'autonomic' is loosely accurate since sacral parasympathetic neurons sit in a similar lateral position; this is a level-of-detail issue, not a clear error.

**447. [MEDIUM · factual · doubtful, low priority] Lecture 07 - The Antebrachium**

- **Where:** Bones of the Antebrachium > Radius
- **Notes say:** "Styloid process increases concavity, surface area contact of wrist joint"
- **Issue:** The radial styloid process does not increase concavity or articular surface area. It is a pointed lateral projection that gives ligament attachment and, by projecting distally, limits radial deviation of the hand. The concavity of the distal radius comes from the carpal articular surface, not the styloid.
- **References say:** The styloid process of the radius provides attachment for ligaments supporting the lateral wrist and projects distally, limiting lateral deviation of the hand. It is not described as increasing concavity or contact area.
- **OpenStax sentence:** "This provides attachment for ligaments that support the lateral side of the wrist joint." (OpenStax A&P 2e §8.2)
- **Basis:** OpenStax §8.2 | **Confidence:** 0.65 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** doubtful: The radial styloid does extend the lateral edge of the distal radial carpal articular surface, so it arguably deepens the concavity and adds contact area, and many texts describe this, though its main roles are ligament attachment and limiting radial deviation, so the flag overstates the error and the notes are arguably acceptable.

**450. [MEDIUM · factual · doubtful, low priority] Lecture 02 - Muscles of the Back**

- **Where:** Intrinsic Back Muscles > Intermediate Group:
- **Notes say:** "Acute strains – associated with sudden extension with excessive loads, often combined with trunk rotation"
- **Issue:** Acute low back strains are classically associated with sudden flexion (or flexion with rotation) under heavy load, such as lifting with a flexed trunk, not sudden extension. The erector spinae work eccentrically and are strained during loaded flexion and rotation.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** doubtful: The notes' statement is arguably consistent with Moore's Clinically Oriented Anatomy, which says back strains occur with sudden excessive extension (hyperextension) of the back, though many other sources describe flexion with rotation under load, so the flag's claim is not clearly right.

**451. [MEDIUM · factual · doubtful, low priority] Lecture 04 - The Shoulder**

- **Where:** Pectoral Region > Clavicle
- **Notes say:** "Structural weak point ²/₃ along the mid-shaft of the clavicle; due to curvature, differences in ossification of regions (medial 2/3 endochondral, lateral ¹/₃ intramembranous)"
- **Issue:** The clavicle is unusual in ossifying mainly by intramembranous ossification in the shaft, with endochondral ossification only at the ends (the medial epiphysis in particular). The claim that the medial two-thirds is endochondral and the lateral third is intramembranous is incorrect. The usual description is that the medial and lateral thirds meet at the weak junction, which is why it fractures there.

- **Second check:** doubtful: Many texts do describe the clavicle's medial two-thirds as ossifying by endochondral ossification and its lateral third by intramembranous ossification, which is the very difference the notes cite for the weak junction, though other sources say the shaft is mainly intramembranous with endochondral ossification only at the ends, so the flag's claim of outright error is contested rather than settled.

**453. [MEDIUM · factual · doubtful, low priority] Lecture 06 - The Brachium**

- **Issue:** The brachial artery runs along the medial border of biceps brachii, but it lies deep to (posterior to) the biceps only proximally and is overlapped by its medial border; in the cubital fossa it lies beneath the bicipital aponeurosis.
