- **Where:** Nerves of the Antebrachium > Radial Nerve
- **Notes say:** "Enters the antebrachium by passing anterior to lateral epicondyle, lying between brachioradialis and extensor carpi radialis longus muscles, supplying them with muscular branches"
- **Issue:** The radial nerve enters the forearm by passing anterior to the lateral epicondyle, lying in the groove between brachialis and brachioradialis (in the cubital fossa), not between brachioradialis and extensor carpi radialis longus. It supplies brachialis (lateral part), brachioradialis and ECRL with branches. Placing it between brachioradialis and ECRL misstates the relation at the point of entry.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** doubtful: Standard teaching is that the radial nerve pierces the lateral intermuscular septum and descends into the cubital fossa in the groove between brachialis and brachioradialis (and ECRL), passing anterior to the lateral epicondyle, where it divides into superficial and deep branches; the notes' phrase 'between brachioradialis and ECRL' is a commonly stated, acceptable description (e.g., the radial nerve lies deep to brachioradialis and ECRL), so the flag's correction is not clearly right and the notes are not clearly wrong, though the detail is somewhat imprecise.

**455. [MEDIUM · factual · doubtful, low priority] Lecture 08 - Anterior and Medial Thigh**

- **Where:** The Lower Limb > Subcutaneous Structures within the Lower Limb > Cutaneous Nerves
- **Notes say:** "both of which branch from the sciatic nerve"
- **Issue:** The medial sural cutaneous nerve branches from the tibial nerve, and the lateral sural cutaneous nerve from the common fibular nerve. Both are branches of the sciatic nerve's divisions, not direct branches of the sciatic trunk. Also, the sural nerve is usually formed by the union of the medial sural cutaneous nerve and the sural communicating branch of the common fibular nerve, rather than by both sural cutaneous nerves.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** doubtful: The medial sural cutaneous nerve arises from the tibial nerve and the lateral sural cutaneous nerve from the common fibular nerve, so they are not direct branches of the sciatic nerve, though both are ultimately sciatic derivatives; the sural nerve is usually formed by the medial sural cutaneous nerve plus the fibular communicating branch (from the lateral sural cutaneous or common fibular nerve), so the notes' wording is imprecise and misleading but arguably acceptable as a loose statement.

**456. [MEDIUM · factual · doubtful, low priority] Lecture 09 - The Hand**

- **Where:** Muscles of the Hand > Hypothenar Eminence > Flexor Digiti Minimi Brevis
- **Notes say:** "Inserts on outside of proximal phalanx"
- **Issue:** Flexor digiti minimi brevis inserts on the medial (ulnar) side of the base of the proximal phalanx of the little finger, not the outside/lateral side. The note's wording is ambiguous and conflicts with the reference.
- **References say:** The reference table lists the insertion of flexor digiti minimi brevis as the medial side of the proximal phalanx of the little finger.
- **OpenStax sentence:** "Medial side of proximal phalanx of little finger" (OpenStax A&P 2e §11.5)
- **Basis:** OpenStax §11.5 | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** doubtful: Standard texts say flexor digiti minimi brevis inserts on the ulnar (medial) side of the base of the proximal phalanx of the little finger, and the notes' 'outside' is consistent with the ulnar/outer border of the hand, though the wording is ambiguous and the notes also use 'lateral (inside border)' confusingly, so the flag's claim of error is arguable rather than clear.

**457. [MEDIUM · factual · doubtful, low priority] Lecture 10 - Anterior and Lateral Leg**

- **Where:** The Leg > Vascular Supply
- **Notes say:** "Just distal to proximal tibiofibular joint, divides into…"
- **Issue:** The popliteal artery divides into the anterior and posterior tibial arteries at the distal border of popliteus, i.e. proximal to (or at the level of) the tibiofibular joint, with the anterior tibial then passing over the top of the interosseous membrane. Saying it divides distal to the proximal tibiofibular joint misplaces the bifurcation.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** doubtful: The popliteal artery typically bifurcates at the distal border of popliteus, roughly at the level of the proximal tibiofibular joint or just distal to it, so the notes' wording is within standard teaching and the flag overstates the discrepancy, though the exact level varies by source.

**458. [MEDIUM · factual · doubtful, low priority] Lecture 10 - Anterior and Lateral Leg**

- **Where:** Anterior Compartment of the Leg > Muscles > Tibialis Anterior
- **Notes say:** "Tendon projects anteriorly away from joint, giving TA significant mechanical advantage"
- **Issue:** The tendon of tibialis anterior crosses the front of the ankle and is held close to the joint by the extensor retinacula, which prevent bowstringing. A tendon projecting away from the joint would be the opposite of what the retinacula do. The statement is misleading as written.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** doubtful: The notes' line is a recognised teaching point: the tibialis anterior tendon passes anterior to the ankle axis and, held by the retinacula, still sits at a moment arm from the axis, so the flag's claim that retinacula make this the opposite is a partial misreading, though 'projects anteriorly away from the joint' is loosely worded and the 'significant mechanical advantage' is arguable.

**459. [MEDIUM · factual · doubtful, low priority] Lecture 11 - Gluteal Region and Posterior Thigh**

- **Where:** Gluteal Region > Superficial Muscle Layer > Tensor Fascia Lata
- **Notes say:** "Fusiform muscle originating off the iliac crest, inferior to the ASIS"
- **Issue:** Tensor fasciae latae arises from the outer lip of the anterior iliac crest and the ASIS itself, not from a point inferior to the ASIS. Saying it originates inferior to the ASIS misstates its origin.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** doubtful: The notes' statement is internally inconsistent, since the iliac crest lies superior to the ASIS, so a muscle originating off the crest cannot be inferior to it; however, the flag's description of the origin (outer lip of the anterior iliac crest and ASIS) matches standard texts, and the notes' wording is a loose, arguably acceptable approximation, perhaps meaning the muscle lies distal to the ASIS.

**460. [MEDIUM · factual · doubtful, low priority] Lecture 11 - Gluteal Region and Posterior Thigh**

- **Where:** Posterior Thigh > Muscles > Biceps Femoris
- **Notes say:** "Convergent muscle with 2 distinct heads"
- **Issue:** Biceps femoris is a fusiform/pennate-type muscle with two heads (long and short), not a convergent (triangular) muscle. Describing it as convergent is a likely error in the muscle architecture classification. Convergent muscles are broad, fan-shaped muscles like pectoralis major or gluteus medius. This conflicts with standard descriptions.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** doubtful: Biceps femoris is commonly classified as a convergent (triangular) muscle in some fascicle-architecture schemes, since its two heads converge on a common tendon at the fibular head, though other sources call it fusiform or bipennate, so the classification is source-dependent and the flag's assertion is not clearly correct.

**461. [MEDIUM · factual · doubtful, low priority] Lecture 12 - Posterior Leg and Plantar Surface of Foot**

- **Where:** Popliteal Fossa > Contents > Popliteal Artery
- **Notes say:** "Supplies the 4 genicular branches that anastamose around the knee"
- **Issue:** The popliteal artery typically gives 5 genicular branches: superior lateral, superior medial, middle, inferior lateral, inferior medial. The count of 4 omits the middle genicular, which pierces the oblique popliteal ligament. Also 'anastamose' is a misspelling.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** doubtful: The popliteal artery gives five genicular branches (superior lateral, superior medial, middle, inferior lateral, inferior medial), but the middle genicular supplies cruciate ligaments and does not take part in the peri-patellar anastomosis, so the notes' '4 genicular branches that anastamose' is a defensible description of the anastomotic ones, though stating 4 without qualification is imprecise; the spelling point is not an anatomical error.

**462. [MEDIUM · factual · doubtful, low priority] Lecture 12 - Posterior Leg and Plantar Surface of Foot**

- **Where:** Posterior Leg > Superficial Compartment > Soleus
- **Notes say:** "Arch pierced by popliteal artery/vein/nerve"
- **Issue:** The tendinous (soleal) arch is passed under by the popliteal vessels and tibial nerve as they enter the deep posterior compartment. The popliteal artery bifurcates at or above the arch, so the structures passing deep to the arch are the posterior tibial artery/vein and tibial nerve. 'Pierced by popliteal artery' is inaccurate, and 'popliteal nerve' is not a standard name (tibial nerve).
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** doubtful: The tendinous arch of soleus is passed under by the popliteal vessels (which become the posterior tibial vessels at the arch, with the popliteal artery terminating at the lower border of popliteus) and the tibial nerve, but the notes' phrase 'popliteal artery/vein/nerve' misnames the structures: the nerve is the tibial nerve (no 'popliteal nerve'), and the structures passing deep to the arch are largely posterior tibial vessels, so the statement is imprecise and arguably misleading.

**463. [MEDIUM · factual · doubtful, low priority] Lecture 12 - Posterior Leg and Plantar Surface of Foot**

- **Where:** Posterior Leg > Tibial Nerve
- **Notes say:** "Injury uncommon, but would produce loss of plantar flexion; patient shows highly modified gait (extreme lateral rotation of hip to avoid unwanted dorsiflexion during stance phase"
- **Issue:** The gait description does not follow from the deficit. Tibial nerve injury paralyzes plantar flexors, so the patient cannot push off and the foot is unwantedly dorsiflexed (calcaneal gait). Hip lateral rotation is not the standard compensation, and the 'avoid unwanted dorsiflexion' rationale is muddled. Loss of plantar flexion also leaves the foot everted/dorsiflexed with weak inversion, so the gait explanation is misleading.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** doubtful: Tibial nerve injury does cause loss of plantar flexion, but the claim that the gait involves extreme lateral hip rotation to avoid unwanted dorsiflexion during stance is nonstandard; the classic picture is a calcaneal gait with weak push-off, though some teaching does describe hip rotation or circumduction compensations, so the note is questionable but not clearly wrong.

**464. [MEDIUM · factual · doubtful, low priority] Lecture 13 - The Hip and Knee Joints**

- **Where:** The Hip Joint > Os Coxae: the Pelvic Bone
- **Notes say:** "Fusion begins in late adolescence; complete by 25 years of age"
- **Issue:** The notes say fusion of the ilium, ischium and pubis only begins in late adolescence. The ossification of the triradiate cartilage starts at about 13-15 years (early to mid adolescence) and the three bones are largely fused by the mid-to-late teens. Completion by 25 is the timing for secondary centers, such as the iliac crest, not for fusion of the three primary bones. The textbook gives the late teenage years for fusion.
- **References say:** The hip bone's three component bones fuse during the late teenage years, and the cartilage between them is replaced by bone when growth stops.
- **OpenStax sentence:** "Each adult hip bone is formed by three separate bones that fuse together during the late teenage years." (OpenStax A&P 2e §8.3)
- **Basis:** OpenStax §8.3 | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** doubtful: Standard texts (e.g., Gray's, Moore) say the three bones fuse at the acetabulum's triradiate cartilage around 14-16 years, completing by about 15-17, while only secondary centers (iliac crest, ischial tuberosity) fuse up to about 20-25; so 'begins in late adolescence, complete by 25' misstates the timing of fusion of the three bones, though some sources loosely cite 20-25 for full os coxae fusion.

**465. [MEDIUM · factual · doubtful, low priority] Lecture 14 - The Pelvic Cavity**

- **Where:** Overview
- **Notes say:** "Organs lie retroperitoneal"
- **Issue:** Pelvic organs are not uniformly retroperitoneal. The bladder, uterus, and upper rectum are covered by peritoneum to varying degrees (subperitoneal/infraperitoneal or partly peritonealized). Only parts such as the lower rectum are extraperitoneal. The blanket statement is misleading.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** doubtful: The notes' blanket claim that pelvic organs lie retroperitoneal is oversimplified, since the pelvic cavity is continuous with the abdominal cavity and the peritoneum drapes over the bladder dome, uterus, and upper rectum, though most pelvic organs are subperitoneal or retroperitoneal in relation to the peritoneal floor, so the statement is misleading but has some defensible basis.

**466. [MEDIUM · factual · doubtful, low priority] Lecture 15 - The Perineum**

- **Where:** Urogenital Triangle > Superficial Perineal Pouch
- **Notes say:** "Tonically contracted to support pelvic floor in standing/sitting positions"
- **Issue:** The superficial transverse perineal muscle is a thin muscle that fixes the perineal body. It is not described as tonically contracted to support the pelvic floor in standing or sitting. Pelvic floor support is the role of the levator ani. This statement looks misleading.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** doubtful: The superficial transverse perineal muscle is a small, variable muscle whose standard role is to stabilize and fix the perineal body, and describing it as tonically contracted to support the pelvic floor in standing/sitting is not a standard teaching, though it contributes modestly to perineal body support, so the claim is largely but not entirely unsupported.

**467. [MEDIUM · factual · doubtful, low priority] Lecture 15 - The Perineum**

- **Where:** Anal Triangle
- **Notes say:** "Space is continuous with deep perineal pouch of urogenital triangle"
- **Issue:** The ischioanal fossa's anterior recess extends forward superior to the perineal membrane, lying lateral/above the deep perineal pouch but separated from it by fascia. Saying it is continuous with the deep pouch is misleading; it is typically described as communicating with the contralateral side behind the anal canal (deep postanal space) and the anterior recess.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** doubtful: The notes say the ischioanal fossa space is continuous with the deep perineal pouch, but standard anatomy says the anterior recess of the fossa extends forward above the perineal membrane, lying beside (not within) the deep pouch and separated from it by fascia; the fossa's recognised continuities are with the contralateral fossa behind the anal canal and, more loosely, the anterior recess, so the statement is misleading.

**468. [MEDIUM · factual · doubtful, low priority] Lecture 16 - Anterolateral Thoracoabdominal Wall**

- **Where:** Slide 09
- **Notes say:** "There is also a prominent 3 tubercle for insertion of the posterior scalene."
- **Issue:** The posterior scalene inserts on the second rib, but the prominent roughness there is for serratus anterior origin (tuberosity for serratus anterior) and the posterior scalene attaches to the outer surface; calling it a 'prominent tubercle for insertion of the posterior scalene' is misleading. The second rib's characteristic feature is the tuberosity for serratus anterior.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** doubtful: The second rib does have a roughened area on its outer surface, the tuberosity for serratus anterior, and the posterior scalene inserts on the second rib's external surface (the standard textbook description is that it inserts on the second rib), so the notes' 'tubercle for posterior scalene' is loosely worded but not clearly wrong, and the flag's own concession that the posterior scalene attaches to the rib partly undermines it.

**469. [MEDIUM · factual · doubtful, low priority] Lecture 16 - Anterolateral Thoracoabdominal Wall**

- **Where:** Slide 12
- **Notes say:** "5 synarthrotic manubriosternal joint"
- **Issue:** The manubriosternal joint is a secondary cartilaginous joint (symphysis), which is classed as amphiarthrotic, not synarthrotic. It may ossify in old age but is not typically described as synarthrotic.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** doubtful: The manubriosternal joint is usually described as a secondary cartilaginous joint (symphysis), which is functionally amphiarthrotic, though some texts call it a synchondrosis or synarthrosis, and it often ossifies with age; the 'synarthrotic' label is arguably acceptable depending on convention but is not the standard classification.

**470. [MEDIUM · factual · doubtful, low priority] Lecture 16 - Anterolateral Thoracoabdominal Wall**

- **Where:** Slide 06
- **Notes say:** "Scarpa’s fascia is composed of dense irregular connective tissue and is therefore more consistent in its thickness"
- **Issue:** Scarpa's fascia is the deep membranous layer of the superficial fascia. It is a thin, membranous layer of loose connective tissue with little fat, not dense irregular connective tissue. The notes' dense irregular description is not standard.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** doubtful: Scarpa's fascia is indeed the deep membranous layer of the superficial fascia, but it is commonly described as a firm fibrous/membranous layer, and some texts do call it dense or irregular connective tissue; calling it 'loose connective tissue' is itself not clearly standard, so the flag's claim is arguable rather than a clear error in the notes.

**471. [MEDIUM · factual · doubtful, low priority] Lecture 16 - Anterolateral Thoracoabdominal Wall**

- **Where:** Slide 09
- **Notes say:** "extend inferiorly from the costal margin and xiphoid process to insert on the pubic crest and symphysis pubis"
- **Issue:** Rectus abdominis originates at the pubic crest and symphysis and inserts superiorly on the xiphoid process and costal cartilages of ribs 5-7. The notes reverse origin and insertion, saying it inserts on the pubis. The direction of description (superior to inferior) is misleading.
- **References say:** R1 says the rectus abdominis originates at the pubic crest and symphysis and extends up the trunk, with insertion on the sternum and ribs 5 and 7.
- **OpenStax sentence:** "These enclose the rectus abdominis muscles (a pair of long, linear muscles, commonly called the “sit-up” muscles) that originate at the pubic crest and symphysis" (OpenStax A&P 2e §11.4)
- **Basis:** OpenStax §11.4 | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** doubtful: Standard references (e.g., Moore) describe the rectus abdominis as attaching superiorly to the xiphoid and costal cartilages 5-7 and inferiorly to the pubic crest and symphysis, with the pubic end often called the origin, so the notes' reversal is a convention about origin vs. insertion that varies between sources and is not a clear factual error.

**472. [MEDIUM · factual · doubtful, low priority] Lecture 16 - Posterior Thigh and Popliteal Fossa**

- **Where:** Posterior Thigh > Muscles > Biceps Femoris
- **Notes say:** "Convergent muscle with 2 distinct heads"
- **Issue:** The biceps femoris is usually described as a bipennate muscle, not convergent. Its long and short heads are distinct, but its fascicle arrangement is pennate. Calling it convergent is likely a wrong fascicle-architecture classification. This is based on standard anatomy and a typical atlas description, and could be a lecture-specific convention, so it needs checking.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** doubtful: Biceps femoris is commonly classified as a convergent (triangular) muscle in many lecture schemes, with two heads converging on the fibular head tendon, whereas the claim that it is 'usually bipennate' is not a standard description and the classification depends on the source used.

**473. [MEDIUM · factual · doubtful, low priority] Lecture 16b - Middle Ear**

- **Where:** III. Middle Ear (Tympanic Cavity) > 3. Walls of the Middle Ear
- **Notes say:** "Vascular structures (Jugular vein and internal carotid artery)."
- **Issue:** The inferior wall (floor) is related to the jugular bulb (internal jugular vein). The internal carotid artery is related to the anterior wall (carotid wall), not the floor. Listing it as part of the inferior wall is misleading.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** doubtful: The floor (jugular wall) of the tympanic cavity is related to the jugular bulb, while the internal carotid artery lies at the anterior (carotid) wall, so listing the carotid under the inferior wall is imprecise, though the notes' broad 'vascular structures' grouping is a loose simplification that some sources tolerate.

**474. [MEDIUM · factual · doubtful, low priority] Lecture 20 - Larynx**

- **Where:** VI. Muscles of the Larynx > F. Thyroepiglottic Muscles
- **Notes say:** "Contraction pulls epiglottis forward - anteriorly."
- **Issue:** The thyroepiglottic muscle runs from the thyroid cartilage to the epiglottis and the aryepiglottic fold. It widens the laryngeal inlet by pulling the epiglottis down/back, assisting closure of the inlet. It does not pull the epiglottis forward. The aryepiglottic and oblique arytenoid muscles act similarly to narrow the inlet. The stated direction is likely wrong.
- **References say:** Not covered by the provided OpenStax text. Verify with a standard reference.
- **Basis:** general anatomy knowledge | **Confidence:** 0.6 | **Severity:** medium
- **Second check:** doubtful: The thyroepiglottic muscle, the continuation of the aryepiglottic fibers, runs from the thyroid lamina to the epiglottis and aryepiglottic fold and is standardly described as widening the laryngeal inlet by drawing the epiglottis downward and backward or tilting it, not pulling it forward; the flag's own description is internally inconsistent, but the notes' claim that it pulls the epiglottis anteriorly is not standard teaching.
